## P1 yielding terrain: definitions fixed before running (2026-11-23, 22:00)

Written before any of the runs below were submitted. Nothing here is a result.

**Sand of the slope tests (open value).** The task's `beach_sand` preset: density 1800 kg/m³, friction
coefficient 0.80, no cohesion (tensile yield ratio 0, yield stress 0), stiff grains (Young's modulus and yield
pressure 1e15), no hardening, dilatancy or viscosity. Bed 0.25 m deep, voxel 0.02 m, cache sand settled 2.5 s.

**Slope-by-friction grid (open value).** Slopes −30°, −20°, −10°, +10°, +20°, +30° (pyramid tile, walking along
+X at 1.0 m/s, 10 trials per cell and policy, same start noise and seed 41 as the slope sweep). Materials:
`beach_sand` with friction 0.80 (its own value), 0.65, 0.50 and 0.35, and as softer materials `poppy_seed_loose`
(640 kg/m³, friction 0.73) and `snow` (250 kg/m³, friction 0.30, compressible, settle limit 0.05 m/s). 36 cells,
every cell reported with goals reached and velocity error. Expectation written down beforehand: a cohesionless
bed should hold on a slope only while friction > tan(angle), so beach sand with friction 0.50 or 0.35 at ±30°
(tan = 0.58) and 0.35 at ±20° (tan = 0.36) may slide on its own; such cells are reported as what they are.

**Stance.** From the first 50 Hz sample in which a foot carries more than 20 N to the last one before it stays
unloaded for three samples. Left out: stances on the flat start platform, stances shorter than 0.1 s, stances
cut off by the end of the trial. A trial enters an average if it has at least three stances on the slope.
"Downhill" is along the slope surface (backward on flat ground).

**Foot motion (measurement 1).** The bearing point of the foot is the lowest of heel, middle and toe of the sole
over the slope surface; its path over a stance is the foot's motion (a foot that rolls about its toe without
slipping scores zero). Split: 0.06 s after touchdown, when the foot has dug in, the sand within 3 cm under the
sole (in the foot's own frame) is marked; until lift-off, (b) the median motion of that sand is "sand under the
foot moving with it", and (a) the bearing point's motion minus (b) is "the foot sliding over the sand". The
foot's motion during the first 0.06 s is reported separately. Displaced volume per stance: volume of sand that
moved more than 1 cm between touchdown and lift-off inside a 47 × 24 cm patch around the footprint, all depths.
Control: the same patch 0.6 m to the outer side of the foot, where no foot presses.

**Foot behaviour (measurement 2).** Stance duration; step length and step time between touchdowns of opposite
feet; angle of the sole to the slope surface at touchdown and in mid-stance; loading from the foot force after
every 5 ms physics step: time from the first step above 20 N to half body weight, and the largest load in the
first 0.1 s.

**Close-up and failure types (measurement 3).** Angle +20°. Trial: the 5th of 10 when sorted by distance along
+X per second of trial (the lower median). Frame: the first one, at least 1.5 m up the slope, in which a foot has
been loaded for 0.1 s; for a trial that ends earlier, the last close-up before it ended. Failure rule: fall with
the pelvis tilted mostly backward, forward or sideways = tips backward, forward, sideways; pelvis too low
without that tilt = sinks or collapses; out of time = slides back if the pelvis moved backward over the last
2 s, stalls if it advanced less than 0.2 m, too slow otherwise; left the lane = leaves the lane.

**Sliding angle of the sand (sanity check 7).** Unloaded bed, gravity tilted in steps of 2.5° and held 2 s at each
step; the sliding angle is the first tilt at which the median speed of the top 5 cm of sand exceeds 1 cm/s.

**Added 2026-11-24, 00:30, before the runs below: material axis and bed depth.** The walking results on soft
sands turned out to be results on the rigid base under the bed (block "Do the loaded feet reach the rigid base
under the sand?"). From now on:

- *Bed depth.* The geometry sweep and the material test run on a 0.35 m bed, which is deeper than the 0.25 m
  bed of training; MPM-Rough is also run on 0.25 m. Every cell reports the base clearance (lowest sole corner
  above the rigid base while loaded), and a cell in which any trial comes within 2 cm is marked invalid.
- *Material axis.* Level ground and +10°, 10 trials per cell and policy, beach sand with density and friction
  replaced by: (3000 kg/m³, 0.7), (2000, 0.9), (2000, 0.7), (2000, 0.5), (1000, 0.7), (500, 0.9), plus beach sand
  itself (1800, 0.80) and hard ground. These are sands in which no trial of the earlier flat grid reached the
  base on a 0.35 m bed. Two lines are read from them: lower friction at 2000 kg/m³ (0.9 → 0.7 → 0.5) and lower
  density at friction 0.7 (3000 → 2000 → 1000 kg/m³). All of these lie inside the training range of MPM-Rough
  (density 1000–3000 kg/m³, friction 0.3–0.9) except (500, 0.9), so this axis does not test out-of-range
  materials. Softness of a sand is reported as the measured foot sinkage from the settled surface.
- *Not results any more.* Snow, clay, loose poppy seed and the sands of the flat grid in which the feet reach
  the base are reported as "feet reach the base", not as material results. The ±30° cells of the
  slope-by-friction grid stay as a stated limit.

**Added 2026-11-24, 02:10, before the final-checkpoint rerun was submitted: bed-depth test, MPM against RFT, and
what "yields less" means.** The independent check (`checks/T3.md`) found that the 2 cm rule above was set after
the results were seen and that a cell which passes it changed threefold when the bed got deeper. The rule is
therefore tested, and these criteria are fixed before any result of the rerun exists:

- *Runs.* MJWarp `3qymp37c`, RFT `6t0h2ud8`, MPM `aga5ovkp` (final checkpoint), all three in every workflow, one
  code bundle for all workflows. Beds of 0.25 m (training depth), 0.35 m and 0.50 m. Slope sweep −30° to +30° in
  5° steps on beach sand, and the six sands of the material axis on level ground and +10°. On 0.25 and 0.35 m:
  10 trials per cell, seed 41. On 0.50 m: 5 trials per run (10 do not fit in the memory of one L40), seeds 41 and
  42, so 10 trials per cell whose start states are drawn from the same distribution but are not the same ten.
  Steep uphill cells (+15°, +20°, +25°, +30°) get 40 trials per policy on 0.35 m (seeds 41 to 44, 10 each) and on
  0.50 m (seeds 41 to 48, 5 each). The flat density-friction grid is repeated on 0.35 m for all three policies.
- *Depth test.* A cell counts as unchanged between 0.35 and 0.50 m if the goal count differs by at most 3 of 10
  (the range seen between three repeats of one cell) and the mean velocity error of successful trials differs by
  at most 0.05 m/s (the largest spread between trials seen so far). The clearance threshold for a valid cell is
  then the smallest clearance on the 0.35 m bed above which every cell is unchanged. If the changed cells are not
  the ones with the smallest clearance, clearance is dropped as a validity rule, results are quoted from the
  deepest bed, and the depth dependence is stated beside them.
- *MPM against RFT.* Compared at +25° and +30° with 40 trials per policy on each bed. "Separated" means Fisher's
  exact test on goals reached gives p < 0.05 (two-sided) on a bed where the cell is valid for both policies.
  Anything else is written as "not separated".
- *"MPM makes the sand yield less".* Tested at +20° and +25°, where both RFT and MPM walk, on the 0.35 m bed:
  sand motion under the sole per stance (median over stances), per seed. It counts as less only if MPM's value
  is below RFT's in every seed and the difference of the medians is larger than RFT's own range between seeds.
- *Base height.* The mesh height is now read under each sole corner, so the clearance is also right on the flat
  start platform and on its edge. Files written before this hold the height under the ankle only; for those the
  clearance uses samples from 0.4 m past the platform edge.
- *Not covered.* One training seed and one checkpoint per policy.

**Added 2026-11-24, 02:29, before the material-ladder runs were submitted: behaviour of each policy from hard ground
to loose poppy seed.** Requested by Junnosuke through the lead: the behaviour comparison of P1 is across materials
on level ground, not across slopes. The steep-slope comparison (RFT against MPM from +25°) is kept in this file
only.

- *Ground.* Level ground, 1.0 m/s forward command, 13 s, the same start noise as the other walking tests. No slope.
- *Materials, from firm to soft.* All sands are cohesionless with rigid grains (Young's modulus and yield pressure
  1e15 Pa, no tensile strength, no hardening, no dilatancy, no viscosity); they differ in density and friction.

  | Ground | Density [kg/m³] | Friction | Inside the MPM training range (1000–3000 kg/m³, 0.3–0.9) |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Hard ground (rigid, MJWarp contact) | – | – | – |
  | Dense sand | 3000 | 0.90 | yes (stiff corner) |
  | Beach sand | 1800 | 0.80 | yes |
  | Light sand (beach sand, lighter and less friction) | 1000 | 0.70 | yes |
  | Packed poppy seed | 682 | 1.07 | no |
  | Loose poppy seed | 640 | 0.73 | no |

  On the figure hard ground comes first and the sands follow in order of measured foot sinkage (mean over the
  three policies on the reported bed), so "softer" is what the feet measured, not the parameter table.
- *Policies.* MJWarp `3qymp37c`, RFT `6t0h2ud8`, MPM `aga5ovkp` at its final checkpoint (iteration 19,999), all
  three in each workflow, the same code bundle as the rest of the final rerun.
- *Bed depth.* 0.35 m (10 trials, seed 41), 0.50 m (5 trials each with seeds 41 and 42) and 0.65 m (4 trials each
  with seeds 41, 42 and 43). Base clearance is reported for every cell. Results are quoted from the 0.50 m bed if
  every cell is unchanged between 0.50 and 0.65 m by the tolerances of the depth test above (3 goals of 10,
  0.05 m/s); otherwise from the 0.65 m bed, with the depth dependence stated.
- *Measures, per policy and ground.* Foot sinkage and sand displaced per step; sole pitch at touchdown and at
  mid-stance; stance duration and double-stance duration; step length and step time; peak normal and tangential
  foot load in body weights, their ratio, and the loading rate; pelvis lean and pelvis height above the surface;
  velocity error. Mean and standard deviation over trials, and the worst step of each trial. Hard ground has the
  foot force at the control rate only (50 Hz), so loads are compared across grounds from 50 Hz samples.
- *What counts as an explanation.* First the premise is checked: do the velocity errors of MJWarp and RFT rise
  from hard ground to the softest material while MPM's stays within 0.05 m/s? If not, that is reported as it is.
  A behaviour quantity is then named as going with the tracking error only if (a) over the six grounds its rank
  correlation with that policy's velocity error is at least 0.8 in size for both MJWarp and RFT, and (b) MPM's
  change in that quantity from hard ground to the softest material is less than half of each baseline's. If no
  quantity passes, the slide says "not explained yet". The earlier observation (baselines shorten their step time
  on sand, MPM keeps about 0.33 s) is tested by the same rule.
- *Added 02:33, still before any ladder result: what a step is.* In the ladder analysis a foot is in stance
  while its vertical load is above 20 % of body weight, and a stance ends after three samples below that. The
  20 N rule of the other reports cannot be used here: in the earlier 0.35 m runs MJWarp kept both feet above 20 N
  for 82 to 95 % of the time on the softest sands (it wades), so no stance ever ended. The share of time with
  both feet above 20 N is reported as its own quantity. Sand displaced per step is taken from the tracker's
  stance (20 N rule) that starts within 0.1 s of the step; a step without one has no value.
- *Added 04:40, after the first ladder runs and before the control runs: settling time and start height.*
  Two things in the test setup turned out to depend on the bed, and both are now controlled in the ladder.
  (1) *Settling time.* The sand template must settle to below 0.03 m/s before a run. With the default 2.5 s
  several ladder cells just missed that on the cluster (0.030–0.031 m/s), so the whole ladder was rerun with 6 s
  ("ladder B"). The bed is at rest during the trials with either time, but after 6 s its surface lies about 1 cm
  lower and the feet sink 10–20 % less; the slope sweep and the material axis use 2.5 s (except the 500 kg/m³
  sand on 0.50 m, 6 s). (2) *Start height.* The robot starts 0.76 m above the level the sand is generated at,
  and the settled surface lies below that by 5–10 cm on the 0.35 m bed, 8–13 cm on 0.50 m and 11–16 cm on
  0.65 m. A deeper bed therefore starts with a larger drop, which is a second difference between bed depths
  besides the depth itself. Control ("ladder C"): the same ladder with 6 s settling and the start lowered by the
  settled offset measured in ladder B for that material and bed, so the pelvis starts 0.76 m above the settled
  surface on every ground, as on hard ground. Ladder C is the reported ladder; the bed rule above (0.50 m if
  unchanged against 0.65 m, else 0.65 m) is applied to it. Ladder B is kept to show what the start drop does.
  Ladder C runs from a second code bundle that differs from the first by the start-height option of
  `eval_walk.py` (default unchanged) and by whatever other sessions changed in the shared checkout since 02:25.
- *Added 05:50, after the recheck of the final rerun and before these runs: steep slopes without the start
  drop.* The recheck (`checks/T3.md`, N3) notes that every slope run starts with a drop of 4 to 10 cm. Control:
  the steep uphill cells (+15°, +20°, +25°, +30°) on the 0.50 m bed with the robot started on the settled
  surface (`--spawn_z -0.098` with 2.5 s settling, `-0.104` with 6 s settling; the offsets measured in the
  finished runs), 40 trials per policy and slope (seeds 41 to 48, 5 each), three policies per workflow. The
  slope result counts as not caused by the drop if, in each of the two settings, MPM against RFT is still
  separated at +30° by Fisher's exact test (p < 0.05) and each policy's goal count at each slope differs from
  the default-start count by at most 12 of 40 (the 3-of-10 tolerance of the depth test). Anything else is
  reported as a change.
- *Added 06:19, after the diagnostic below and before the runs it leads to: two bed configurations.* The recheck
  (`checks/T3.md`, N1, N2, N8) asks what makes the sand stop yielding and asks for one setting per comparison.
  Diagnostic (local, MJWarp on dense sand, 0.50 m bed, default start, 2 trials of 7 s; `logs/t3/settle_diag/`,
  `logs/t3/settle_probe/`):

  | Settling time | Settled surface below the generated level | Task's sleeping: sinkage, sand displaced per step | All sand near the robot awake |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 2.5 s | 8.2 cm | 4.0 cm, 16.7 L | 2.4 cm, 3.6 L |
  | 6 s | 9.1 cm | 0.8 cm, 2.0 L | 2.1 cm, 2.8 L |
  | 12 s | 9.7 cm | 0.6 cm, 0.9 L | 1.9 cm, 2.9 L |
  | 24 s | 10.0 cm | 0.6 cm, 0.8 L | 1.7 cm, 2.1 L |

  Two things act together. (1) *The bed keeps losing height while it is awake.* The settling check (largest
  speed below 0.03 m/s) does not mean it has stopped: dense sand goes on sinking from 8.2 to 10.0 cm between
  2.5 and 24 s, beach sand from 9.7 to 11.5 cm between 2.5 and 12 s, and low-friction sands collapse (1000 kg/m³
  with friction 0.3: 21 cm after 2.5 s and 27 cm after 12 s of a 50 cm bed; friction 0.1: 30 cm after 2.5 s, and
  after 12 s the template check reports that the bed collapsed onto the floor). (2) *Sleeping freezes the bed in
  the state it has when the settling stops, and sleeping sand answers a foot differently from awake sand.* On a
  bed frozen early the foot wakes loose sand that then compacts under it: more sinkage than with awake sand and
  five times the displaced volume. On a bed frozen late there is little left to compact and the sand that stays
  asleep gives a third of the awake sinkage. With the sand awake the result hardly depends on the settling time.
  The start matters as well: on dense sand with 2.5 s settling the feet sink 4.0 cm after the default drop and
  2.5 cm when the robot is set on the surface; on beach sand 8.3 and 8.2 cm. So "sand that does not yield" is
  sleeping sand on a bed that had more time to compact; sleeping alone does not explain it, and no setting is
  neutral. Whether a bed should lose height like this is a question for the solver and the T1 session.
- *The two configurations, fixed before running.* Both: 0.50 m bed, robot started 0.76 m above the settled
  surface (each task first measures where its sand settles), settling limit 0.06 m/s so that every sand passes,
  velocity error from 1.0 s over the trials that reach the goal, three policies per workflow.
  **T, task setting**: 2.5 s settling and the task's sleeping, which is what the trainings with the tile cache
  and the slope sweep use. **W, awake reference**: 12 s settling and every particle in the 1.2 m box around the
  pelvis awake. Runs: the material ladder (10 trials per cell, seeds 41 and 42) and the density-friction grid
  with every sand generated and settled as itself (5 trials per sand, seed 41) in T and in W; the steep uphill
  slopes (+15° to +30°, 40 trials) in W (T is the no-drop control defined above). A material or slope statement
  is made only where T and W agree: goal counts within 3 of 10 (12 of 40) and velocity errors within 0.05 m/s.
  Where they differ, both numbers are shown and the statement is not made. A sand whose bed collapses while
  settling (surface more than a third of the bed depth below the generated level, or the template check fails)
  is reported as "bed collapses", not as a material result.
- *Added 06:31: sampling density is a condition of every sinkage and sand-motion number.* Every walking run of
  T3 generates the sand with 1.25 particles per cell edge (field `particles_per_cell` of each result file; voxel
  0.02 m). The T1 session's finding, unchecked (`outputs/T1.md`, "Why the settled bed keeps sinking, and what it
  means for sleeping"): at that density with the position jitter used, 12 to 14 % of the grid cells start empty
  and sand moves into them, so the bed compacts; it stays put from about 1.6 particles per cell edge. Foot
  sinkage, sand displaced per step, sand motion under the sole and the settled-surface offsets in this file are
  therefore numbers for 1.25 particles per cell edge, together with the settling time, the sleeping setting and
  the start height stated with each result. The T3 session does not change the sampling density, the wake margin
  or the transfer scheme; that needs Junnosuke's decision.

**Added 2026-11-24, 12:59, before computing anything: sand flow under the foot across materials (P1-2).** Requested
by Junnosuke through the lead: does more sand flow out from under the loaded foot as the ground goes from hard
ground to loose poppy seed, does it differ between the policies, and does a policy's degradation follow it?

- *Data.* The material ladder already run in setting W (headline: 12 s settling, sand near the robot awake) and
  in setting T (comparison), 0.50 m bed, start on the settled surface, 10 trials per policy and ground, level
  ground; hard ground from the rigid run. No new simulation: every run saved, per trial, the particles in a box
  of 1.2 m by 0.5 m around the pelvis at one moment in mid-walk and at the end of the trial, each with its
  displacement since it entered the simulated window (the settled bed), and per stance the motion of the sand
  under the sole. Sand generated with 1.25 particles per cell edge; beds do not collapse on the ladder's sands.
- *Footprint column.* For every completed stance whose footprint lies inside a saved box: the vertical column
  under the sole rectangle (19 cm by 7 cm, as the sole stands at mid-stance, projected onto the ground), through
  the whole bed. A stance is a foot carrying more than 20 % of body weight, as in the ladder analysis.
- *Rut volume* = sole area times the drop of the sand top in the column (97th percentile of particle height in
  the column, before against after).
- *Flow* = the volume of sand that left the column sideways: particles in the column before minus particles in
  it after, times the particle volume. This is the sand that moved aside (and comes up beside the foot).
- *Compaction* = rut volume minus flow: the part of the rut that no sand left, so the same sand takes less
  height (density rise under the foot). On a bed sampled with 1.25 particles per cell edge this is the part that
  the sampling can produce by itself. The same two numbers are taken in columns of the same size that no foot
  touched (at least 12 cm from every footprint of the trial), as the background of the bed.
- *Cross-check of the flow*: the sand gained by the 8 cm band around the footprint and the rise of its surface.
- *Motion of the sand under the sole* (counterpart of the slope figure): the sand that lay within 3 cm under the
  sole once the foot had dug in (0.06 s after touchdown), followed until lift-off: its mean vertical motion and
  its mean horizontal motion. These are averages over all the sand under the sole, not the motion at one contact
  point. Outward motion is taken from the saved boxes: for the top 3 cm of the footprint column, the mean
  horizontal motion away from the footprint's centre line.
- *Beside these*: goals, velocity error (from 1.0 s, trials that reach the goal), sole angle at mid-stance,
  sinkage per stance from the settled surface.
- *Criterion for "degradation follows flow", fixed now.* (1) A policy degrades on the ladder if it fails to reach
  a goal on some ground or its velocity error there is more than 0.05 m/s above its error on hard ground;
  otherwise there is nothing to explain for that policy and that is what is reported. (2) For a policy that
  degrades: over the six grounds, rank the degradation (velocity error; a ground where it falls in every trial
  ranks worst) and the flow per stance; "follows flow" needs a rank correlation of 0.8 or more in setting W and
  in setting T. (3) Between policies, on every sand where one policy's error is more than 0.05 m/s above
  another's or it falls and the other does not, the worse policy must also move more sand by flow per stance, in
  both settings. (4) The same two tests are run with sinkage and with compaction in place of flow. If they pass
  equally, the answer is that flow cannot be told apart from sinkage as the explanation; if flow fails (2) or
  (3), the answer is that flow does not explain the degradation. Whatever comes out is reported.
- *Added 13:07, before the rerun: the saved boxes are not enough, the footprints are measured in the run.* The
  saved boxes hold 0 to 3 footprints per cell that the foot has already left, because sand is simulated only
  within 0.8 m of the pelvis and a box reaches 0.6 m back. No result was drawn from them. The ladder is rerun in
  W and in T (same grounds, bed, start, seeds and policies; third bundle plus the change below) with the tracker
  extended: at each touchdown (first sample above 20 N) it remembers the sand within 0.35 m of the sole, and of
  an untouched place 0.35 m to the outer side; when the same foot lands again, or 0.5 s after lift-off, it
  compares that sand with how it lay at touchdown. Rut volume, flow, compaction, band values and the motion of
  the column's top layer are as defined above, with "before" = at touchdown and "after" = once the foot has
  left; sand more than 6 cm above the column's earlier top (in the air or riding on the foot) is not counted as
  surface. A foot that never unloads below 20 N (MJWarp when it wades) gives no footprint; that is reported as
  such. The criterion above is unchanged.
- *Added 13:15, before any result of the rerun: a sand budget around the footprint.* A local test of the
  extended tracker (RFT on light sand, 2 trials; not a result) showed that sand leaves the footprint column
  sideways while the surface next to the footprint sinks instead of rising, so the column alone cannot say where
  the sand went. The rerun therefore also takes a budget over the sole rectangle grown by 10 cm on every side
  (39 cm by 27 cm; the other foot lands outside it): the sand top is mapped on 2.5 cm cells at touchdown and
  once the foot has left, and *sank* = volume of the cells whose top dropped, *rose* = volume of the cells whose
  top came up, *left* = sand that left the region sideways (particle count), *compaction* = sank − rose − left,
  the volume the same sand no longer takes up. Junnosuke's "flow" is rose + left; the column's flow (sand that
  left the footprint column) is kept beside it. The same budget is taken in a region of the same size 0.35 m to
  the outer side that no foot touches, over the same time: the bed's own change, which is subtracted where
  stated. The flow used in the criterion above is the sand that left the footprint column, as fixed there; the
  budget is reported beside it and tested the same way. Workflows `t3-final-flow2W-s41/42-1-1` and
  `t3-final-flow2T-s41/42-1-1` (fourth bundle, `bundles/1a9aaf78bfb030a3`); `t3-final-flowW/T-…` were submitted
  minutes earlier without the budget and are used only as a repeat of the column values.
- *Added 14:32, after the results and after the fourth check (corrections to the three entries above; they are
  left as written).* (a) *Timing.* The entries of 12:59, 13:07 and 13:15 were written in this file before the
  runs were downloaded (14:06 to 14:11), but the file was first committed with them at 14:18, after the results,
  and `material_flow.py`, which holds the criterion, was last saved at 14:14 (and again after the check, for
  labels, the settled-bed values and the list of grounds behind each number). The order cannot be proven from
  the files. (b) *Unit.* "Flow" in litres is a particle count times the particle volume at the material's nominal
  density: a measure of mass. The settled bed is 1.2 to 1.5 times its nominal density, so the volume of bed that
  left is smaller by that factor and the compaction larger by the same litres. Every flow value is now labelled
  "litres at nominal density"; the value as volume of the settled bed (particle count times sole area times sand
  height over the bed base, per particle in the column at touchdown) is reported beside it. (c) *The region to
  the side* is not "untouched" and its change is not "the bed's own change": no foot lands in it, but in W it
  sinks 4 to 8 mm as the robot passes and in T it receives sand from the footprint. Values are reported with and
  without it taken off. (d) *The budget* sank = rose + left + compaction closes by construction, because
  compaction is the remainder; there is no residual to test. (e) *"Over the six grounds"* in part (2) of the
  criterion: a ground without a footprint (the policy falls or wades in every trial) has no flow value and drops
  out, so the correlations run over the grounds that have one; the grounds behind each number are stated with
  it. (f) *Reading of the last sentence of the criterion.* Part (2) only tests whether a policy's error rises
  with softness: flow and sinkage have the same rank order over the grounds, so it gives the same value for
  both, and with 4 or 5 grounds it has little power. Part (3) cannot be run on the grounds where MJWarp falls
  in every trial. A failed test is therefore reported as "not shown, and not separable from sinkage", not as
  "flow does not explain the degradation".

**Added 2026-11-25, 01:23, before computing anything from the joint records: whole-body comparison of the MPM and the
RFT policy (C-1).** Requested by Junnosuke through the lead: is there something beyond the feet (hip, knee,
whole-body joint data, applied torque, policy action) that explains why MPM does better than RFT? No new
runs: every rollout of the final rerun already stores joint positions, joint velocities, applied torques and
actions of all 29 joints, the base state and the feet at the control rate (50 Hz), and the sand's force on each
foot after every physics step. Up to now only feet, base position and velocity error were read from these
files; the joint, torque and action arrays have not been looked at.

- *Conditions.* (i) Uphill on beach sand, +20°, +25° and +30°, 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, 40 trials per policy and
  slope (seeds 41 to 48, 5 starts each, the same starts for the three policies). Two bed settings carry the
  rule: T (start on the settled surface, 2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping; `surf50`) and W (12 s settling,
  sand kept awake; `steepW`). The default start with the drop (`steep50`) and 6 s settling (`surf50six`) are
  reported beside them and are used for the test inside RFT's trials. (ii) Level ground on the five sands of
  the material ladder, 10 trials per cell, settings T (`ladderT`) and W (`ladderW`). Hard ground has no joint
  record (the hard-ground test stored base and feet only) and enters only for base and foot quantities.
  Policies: MPM `aga5ovkp` against RFT `6t0h2ud8`; MJWarp `3qymp37c` is tabulated for reference and is not
  part of the rule.
- *Window.* On a slope: samples with the pelvis at least 0.3 m past the edge of the start platform; on level
  ground from 1.5 s. Of a trial that does not reach the goal the last 0.5 s are left out. A trial enters with
  at least 3 complete gait cycles in the window.
- *Stance, touchdown, gait cycle.* The load of a foot is the sand's force on it normal to the ground (50 Hz
  record). Touchdown: first sample above 20 % of body weight after at least 3 samples below. Lift-off: last
  sample above it before 3 samples below. Gait cycle of a leg: touchdown to its next touchdown, kept if it
  lasts 0.25 to 1.2 s, resampled to 50 phase points. Cycles of both legs are used; for the right leg the roll
  and yaw joints are mirrored in sign so that both legs overlay. A trial's value is the mean over its cycles
  (or stances); a cell's value is the mean and standard deviation over trials.
- *Quantities, all fixed now* (about 70 numbers per trial).
  1. Leg joints (hip pitch, hip roll, hip yaw, knee, ankle pitch, ankle roll): angle at mid-stance; range over
     the cycle; RMS velocity; RMS applied torque; mean positive mechanical power (torque times velocity, positive
     part). Peak torque (95th percentile of the magnitude) for hip pitch, knee and ankle pitch.
  2. Waist (yaw, roll, pitch) and arms (shoulder pitch, shoulder roll, elbow): mean angle and range over the
     cycle.
  3. Energy: positive mechanical work of all joints per metre travelled; share of it done by the knee and by the
     hip pitch joint.
  4. Policy output: RMS change of the action per control step, for the leg joints and for waist and arms; RMS
     difference between joint target and joint position for hip pitch, knee and ankle pitch (target = default
     joint position + 0.25 times the action, the task's action scaling; default positions from the robot
     configuration, checked against the first sample of the trials).
  5. Base: height of the pelvis above the settled surface; pitch (mean) and roll (RMS about its mean); RMS of
     pitch rate, roll rate, yaw rate and of the velocity normal to the ground.
  6. Velocity error taken apart (arithmetic, not an explanation by itself): mean forward speed minus the
     command; RMS of the forward speed about its trial mean; RMS of the sideways speed.
  7. Pelvis over the stance foot at mid-stance: forward and sideways offset of the pelvis from the sole centre,
     in the walking frame (the pelvis stands in for the centre of mass, which is not recorded).
  8. Foot placement at touchdown relative to the pelvis: forward and sideways; step length and step width
     between successive touchdowns of opposite feet.
  9. Swing: largest height of the sole above the settled surface during swing.
  10. Timing: stance duration, step time, share of time with both feet loaded; time from 20 N to half body
      weight read from the physics-rate force; peak load in body weights.
  11. Reaction to a slip. Slip event: in a stance, the first sample at which the foot carries at least half the
      body weight and the sole centre moves downhill (backward on level ground) faster than 0.15 m/s. Reported:
      share of stances with a slip event; change of hip pitch, knee and ankle pitch angle and of their torques
      over the 0.2 s after the event, relative to the event sample.
- *Comparison.* MPM minus RFT per cell, with trials paired by start (same seed and start index) where both
  policies have a value. Effect size d = mean difference divided by the pooled standard deviation over trials of
  the two policies; the paired version (mean of the paired differences over their standard deviation) is given
  beside it.
- *Rule for calling a quantity an explanation, fixed now.* Two parts, both needed.
  (a) *It separates the two policies:* |d| is at least 2 with the same sign in T and in W, at each of +20°,
  +25° and +30° for the slope case, or on each of the five sands for the material case. Two policies are
  different controllers and trials on one bed spread little, so many quantities are expected to pass (a); by
  itself (a) explains nothing.
  (b) *It goes with the outcome.* Slope case: among RFT's own trials at +30° in the three settings with the
  task's sleeping (default, T, 6 s; 120 trials), using only the first 4 gait cycles on the slope so that the
  fall itself is not measured, the trials that later fall differ from those that reach the goal in the
  direction in which RFT differs from MPM: probability that a falling trial lies further on RFT's side than a
  successful one (area under the ROC curve) at least 0.70 pooled, and above 0.5 in at least two of the three
  settings. Material case: within a policy, over its 50 trials on the five sands of a setting, the rank
  correlation between the quantity and the trial's velocity error has the sign of the difference between the
  policies and a size of at least 0.5 for at least one of the two policies, in T and in W, and the other policy
  does not show the opposite sign with a size of 0.5 or more.
  A quantity that passes (a) and (b) is called an explanation candidate, not a cause. For the material case a
  candidate is then tested on the density-friction grid (14 sands whose bed holds, 5 trials each, T and W):
  the sign of MPM minus RFT has to hold on at least 12 of the 14 sands in both settings.
- *What is reported whatever comes out:* how many quantities were tested, how many pass (a), how many pass (b),
  the full table, and the phase-aligned curves of the leg joints and their torques for MPM and RFT. "Nothing
  passes" is a possible result and is then stated as such. Group 6 is reported as arithmetic and is not
  counted among the candidates.
- *Added 2026-11-25, 01:33, after computing (what was changed against the entry above, which is left as written).*
  (1) A fault in my computation, found on the first run by its implausible values and corrected before any
  result was written up: the result files store the pelvis position relative to the trial's own tile in x and
  y and the feet in world coordinates, so the pelvis-over-foot and foot-placement numbers were wrong; the pelvis
  is now put into the feet's frame. It changed those four quantities only (one more quantity then separates on
  the slopes, 24 instead of 23) and no verdict. (2) One test was added after seeing that two quantities passed
  the rule on the sands: the rank correlation with the velocity error within each sand, over its trials. It is
  not part of the rule fixed beforehand and is reported as an addition. (3) Counts: 79 quantities came out of
  the list above, not "about 70". (4) Hard ground entered only the velocity-error arithmetic.

**Added 2026-11-25, 01:35, before computing anything from the last seconds before RFT's falls (follow-up of the
whole-body comparison, asked by the lead).** The whole-body comparison left out the last 0.5 s of every failing
trial and tested only the first four gait cycles on the slope. This follow-up looks at what was left out. No
new runs; the same result files. Nothing of the time course before a fall has been looked at so far, apart
from Figure W1 (first four gait cycles) and the earlier failure counts.

- *Data.* Beach sand at +30° (and +25° for the slip part), 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, 40 trials per policy and
  setting: default start, T (start on the surface, 2.5 s settling), 6 s settling, and W (sand kept awake). A
  fall is a trial that ends by a termination of the task (pelvis too low or tilted too far); trials that end by
  leaving the lane or running out of time are counted and left out. The fall time is the last recorded sample
  of the trial, its place the pelvis position along the walk at that sample.
- *Windows.* Fall window: the 2.0 s before the fall, in bins of 0.1 s counted back from the fall (bin 1 is the
  last 0.1 s). Control windows for one fall, matched by distance up the slope: for every RFT trial of the same
  setting that reaches the goal, and for every MPM trial of the same setting, the 2.0 s that end when its
  pelvis first passes the place of the fall. The controls of one fall are averaged, so each fall has one RFT
  control profile and one MPM profile. Falls with less than 2.0 s on record before them keep the bins they
  have. In W only one RFT trial reaches the goal, so W enters the comparison with MPM only.
- *Quantities per bin* (mean over the samples of the bin): pelvis pitch forward [deg] and pitch rate; forward
  speed; pelvis height above the settled surface; downhill speed of the sole centre of a foot that carries at
  least half the body weight; share of time with both feet loaded and with no foot loaded (20 % of body
  weight); RMS of the action over the leg joints and its largest magnitude; RMS of target minus position over
  the leg joints; for each leg joint the applied torque as a share of its effort limit (hip pitch 88, hip roll
  139, hip yaw 88, knee 139, ankle pitch and ankle roll 50 N m), the larger of the two legs, and the share of
  samples at or above 95 % of the limit; the same for joint speed against the speed limit (32, 20, 32, 20, 37,
  37 rad/s). The task sets no clip on the action, so "clipping" is read from torque and speed against their
  limits. *Per stance in the window* (stances that end in it): sand motion under the sole downhill (the
  tracker's median over the sand within 3 cm under the sole), pelvis ahead of the stance sole at mid-stance,
  foot ahead of the pelvis at touchdown, step length and step time.
- *Question 1: what diverges first, and how early?* For every quantity and bin, the difference between the
  falls and their RFT controls, divided by the pooled standard deviation over trials (d). A quantity has
  diverged at bin k if |d| is at least 1 in bin k and in every later bin up to the fall, with one sign. Its
  divergence time is the earliest such bin, pooled over the three settings with the task's sleeping, and it
  counts only if at least two of the three settings give the same sign at that bin. Reported: the divergence
  time of every quantity, ordered; "first" is the earliest.
- *Question 2: is an actuator at its limit before the falls?* Yes for a joint if, in the last 1.0 s before the
  falls, its torque (or speed) is at or above 95 % of the limit in at least 10 % of the samples, and that share
  is at least twice the share in the RFT control windows.
- *Question 3: does MPM keep more margin at +30°?* Over the control windows (so at the same places on the
  slope): the 95th percentile of torque over limit and of speed over limit per leg joint, of the pelvis pitch,
  and the 5th percentile of the forward speed, for RFT trials that reach the goal and for MPM. Reported as
  numbers with the spread over trials; "more margin" is said for a quantity only if the two differ by at least
  2 pooled standard deviations in the three settings with the task's sleeping and in W for MPM against all of
  RFT's trials.
- *Question 4: is there a trigger that both policies meet and only MPM recovers from?* Slip event as before:
  in a stance, the first sample at which the foot carries at least half the body weight and the sole centre
  moves downhill faster than 0.15 m/s. Its size is how far the sole centre moves downhill in the 0.1 s after
  that sample: small (under 2 cm), medium (2 to 5 cm), large (over 5 cm). Missed step: a step time above 1.5
  times the trial's median step time. For each class of event, at +25° and +30° in every setting: events per
  metre climbed for RFT and for MPM; share of events followed by a fall within 1.0 s; share of falls that
  have such an event in their last 1.0 s against the share of RFT control windows that have one. A class is
  called a trigger only if at least 60 % of the falls have it in their last 1.0 s, at most 30 % of the RFT
  control windows have it, and MPM meets it at least half as often per metre as RFT without falling. *Recovery:*
  for slip events of one size class, the change over the next 0.3 s of pelvis pitch, of the torque of hip
  pitch, knee and ankle pitch of the slipping leg, and of the pelvis position over that foot, for RFT events
  followed by a fall within 1.0 s, RFT events not followed by one, and MPM events.
- *Status of this follow-up.* It is a search in the trials that failed, started after the planned comparison
  found nothing, so whatever it finds is exploratory: a description of how RFT's falls unfold, to be tested on
  runs it was not found in before it is called an explanation. "Nothing diverges before the last half second"
  and "no trigger" are possible results and are then reported as such.
- *Added 2026-11-25, 01:41, after computing the follow-up (what differs from the entry above, which is left as
  written).* (1) The effect size uses the spread of the single control windows. My first run took the spread of
  the per-fall averages of the controls, which understates it and gave divergence times 0.1 to 0.3 s earlier
  (height 0.9 s instead of 0.8 s); the corrected values are the ones reported. (2) The slip classes fixed
  beforehand turned out not to discriminate at +30°: a slip of more than 5 cm in 0.1 s comes at the start of
  almost every loaded stance of both policies. The classes were kept as written and that is reported. (3) No
  per-setting threshold other than the sign rule was added.

**Added 2026-11-25, 01:44, before any run: does the MPM policy need its knee torque to climb? (causal test, asked by
the lead for Junnosuke).** The follow-up above left one candidate: right after touchdown the MPM policy pushes
with the knee up to the knee's torque limit (95th percentile 0.98 of the limit at +30°), about four times RFT's
rise in knee torque, and its pelvis pitches forward half as much per stance. This test takes that torque away
and looks whether MPM then pitches over.

- *What is changed.* The torque limit of both knee joints, and nothing else: same policy (MPM `aga5ovkp`,
  final checkpoint), same observations, same gains. The limit is set in the robot configuration before the
  simulation is built, so that the simulator's actuator force range carries it (the MuJoCo solver turns the
  effort limit into the actuator's force range; it has no joint speed limit). Each run records the limit as
  read back from the simulation, and the largest knee torque that occurred.
- *Steps.* Knee torque limit as a share of the configured 139 N m: 0.85 (118 N m), 0.75 (104 N m), 0.64
  (89 N m, the 95th percentile of what RFT uses at +30° in trials that reach the goal) and 0.50 (70 N m, one
  step below). A control at 1.00 with the new code, 10 trials, has to reproduce the uncapped result (40 of 40
  in the merged runs).
- *Conditions.* Beach sand, 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, the default bed setting of the slope sweep (start with the
  drop, 2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping). +30°: all four steps, 40 trials each (seeds 41 to 48, 5 starts
  each, the starts of the merged runs). +25°: steps 0.64 and 0.50, 40 trials each. Footprint tracker on, so
  the sand motion under the sole is measured as before. OSMO, L40, one small workflow per seed and step.
- *Outcome.* Goals of 40 per step; kind of failure (pelvis tilted too far forward, backward or sideways,
  pelvis too low, lane, time) with the rule of the slope explanation.
- *Reading, fixed now.* (a) *The cap has to bind:* at a step, the knee torque is at or above 95 % of the capped
  limit in at least 10 % of the samples on the slope; a step where it does not bind tests nothing and is
  reported as such. (b) *"The knee push is necessary for MPM's success at +30°"* if at 0.64 MPM reaches at most
  32 of 40 goals (separated from the uncapped 40 of 40, Fisher's exact test p below 0.01), the goals do not
  rise again at 0.50, and most of the failures are the pelvis tilting too far forward. (c) *"Not necessary"* if
  at 0.64 MPM still reaches at least 37 of 40 while the cap binds. (d) Between 33 and 36 of 40: undecided.
  (e) Whatever the verdict, reported beside it: goals at every step (is there a threshold), MPM at 0.64
  against RFT uncapped (13 of 40 in this setting), and the same at +25°.
- *Sand under the sole.* For every step: the sand motion under the sole per stance (median over the sand
  within 3 cm under the sole, dug-in to lift-off; failed trials included up to the fall), against the uncapped
  MPM (5.1 cm at +30°, 3.3 cm at +25°) and RFT (6.4 and 4.5 cm). Stated as a number with its spread; no rule.
- *What a result would and would not mean.* The policy was trained with the full limit. If it fails under the
  cap, that shows that this policy, as trained, needs that torque to climb, which is what "necessary for its
  success" means here; it does not show that no policy could climb with less. If it still climbs, the knee
  push is not what keeps it up, and the candidate is dropped.
- *Second test, only if the first is running and it is cheap:* RFT and MPM at +25°, pushed forward at the
  pelvis by a fixed change of velocity at a fixed moment (the first touchdown after the pelvis is 1.5 m up the
  slope), to compare how far the pelvis pitches and how the knee torque answers in the next 0.3 s, and how many
  trials fall within 2 s. Its sizes and reading are written here before it is run, or it is not run.
- *Added 2026-11-25, 01:48, before the runs were submitted (additions to the entry above).* (1) Asked by the lead: the
  real G1's published maximum knee torque is 90 and 120 N m (two actuator variants), below the 139 N m of the
  simulation, so the steps 118 N m (0.85) and 89 N m (0.64) are also "about the real knee's limit", and the
  0.64 step is run at +20° as well (40 trials), to say what the MPM policy does on the slopes of the hardware
  test with a real knee. No reading rule is attached to +20°; goals and failure kinds are reported. (2) Local
  check before submitting, level ground, one trial, 120 steps, knee limit 40 N m: the articulation data and the
  solver's joint force range both read 40 N m for the two knees (the MuJoCo solver clamps the summed actuator
  force of a joint to that range) and the recorded knee torque does not exceed it. The solver has no joint
  speed limit. The three earlier smoke files are not results and are not used.
- *Added 2026-11-25, 02:53, after the runs (what was done; the rule above is unchanged).* 50 workflows on OSMO pool
  `isaac-lab-l40-07` (L40), all completed at the first attempt: `t3-knee-cnone-a30-s41/42-1` (control),
  `t3-knee-c118/c104/c89/c70-a30-s41…48-1`, `t3-knee-c89-a20x25-s41…48-1`, `t3-knee-c70-a25-s41…48-1`; bundle
  `bundles/b78f0aac4b190e3e` (Isaac Lab `0007b2e67` with uncommitted changes, among them the new option
  `eval_walk.py --knee_torque_limit`; Newton `48231a98`; Warp `3d0f4e5b`). Every run's record of the knee limit
  read back from the simulation equals the requested value, and the largest knee torque in each cell equals the
  cap. The uncapped reference at 139 N m is the merged default run (`steep50`, 40 trials, earlier bundle); the
  control with the new code reproduces it at +30° (10 of 10). Data `logs/t3/final_1/knee/c<limit>`, raw
  downloads `logs/t3/flow_dl/t3-knee-*`, report `scripts/benchmarks/t3/knee_cap_report.py` →
  `logs/t3/final_1/knee/report/knee_cap.json`. The second test (push at the pelvis) was not run.

**Added 2026-11-25, 02:54, before any run: the three policies with about the real knee's torque (asked by the lead).**
The knee test above showed that the MPM policy's success at +30° depends on the simulated knee limit of
139 N m, which is above the real robot's published 90 N m (G1) and 120 N m (G1 EDU). The comparison between
the policies on steep slopes is therefore repeated with the knee limited to 118 N m and to 89 N m.

- *Runs.* RFT `6t0h2ud8` and MJWarp `3qymp37c` with the knee torque limit at 118 and at 89 N m, at +20°, +25°
  and +30°; MPM `aga5ovkp` at 118 N m at +20° and +25° (its other cells exist from the knee test). 40 trials per
  cell: seeds 41 to 48, 5 starts each, the starts of the merged runs and of the MPM knee runs; beach sand,
  0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, default bed setting of the slope sweep; footprint tracker on. Same option
  (`--knee_torque_limit`), same code bundle as the MPM knee runs if it can be reused. The cells at 139 N m are
  the merged default runs (40 trials per policy and slope).
- *Reported per policy, limit and slope:* goals of 40 and kinds of failure; velocity error over the trials that
  reach the goal (mean and standard deviation over trials); share of the samples on the slope with a knee at
  or above 95 % of the limit (does the limit bind for this policy); sand motion under the sole per stance.
- *Question:* with about the real knee's torque, does MPM still do better than RFT on steep slopes, and at
  which slope?
- *Reading per limit (118, 89 N m) and slope, fixed now.* Success: "MPM better" if it reaches more goals than
  RFT and Fisher's exact test on the two counts of 40 gives p below 0.01; "RFT better" the same the other way;
  otherwise "not separated". Velocity error, only where both reach at least 10 goals: "MPM tracks better" if
  its mean error is lower by at least 0.05 m/s and by at least 2 pooled standard deviations over trials; "RFT
  tracks better" the other way; otherwise "not separated".
- *Statement per limit.* "MPM still does better on steep slopes" if MPM is better in success on at least one
  slope and RFT on none. "The advantage in success is gone" if neither is better in success on any slope; it
  is then said whether an advantage in velocity error remains and where. "Reversed" if RFT is better in success
  on at least one slope and MPM on none. Anything else is reported slope by slope without a summary word.
- *MJWarp* is tabulated beside them; it reaches no goal from +20° with the full limit, so no reading is
  attached to it.
- *Stated with the result whichever way it comes out:* none of the policies was trained with the lowered
  limit, so this says how these three policies would do with a weaker knee, not which training method would be
  better if all three were trained for it.

**Added 2026-11-25, 03:05, before any run: two control runs for the knee test (asked by the lead after check 8).**
The knee test showed that the MPM policy as trained needs knee torque above 89 N m to climb +30°. Two things
are open: whether that is special to the knee, and whether the comparison of the three policies depends on
the ankle torque limit, which the simulation sets to 50 N m while Unitree's description files (URDF, MJCF)
list 35 N m.

- *Taken from the existing uncapped runs before writing this rule, to choose and to read the steps (default
  bed setting, 40 trials per policy and slope, samples on the slope, larger of left and right).* Share of the
  MPM policy's samples at +30° in which the torque is above 0.85, 0.75, 0.64 and 0.50 of the joint's limit:
  knee (139 N m) 9.5, 12.6, 17.1, 25.3 %; hip pitch (88 N m) 4.8, 10.8, 20.5, 44.4 %. 95th percentile of the
  hip pitch torque at +30°: MPM 74 N m, RFT 71 N m. Share of samples with the ankle pitch torque at or above
  95 % of 35 N m at +20°, +25°, +30°: MPM 0.1, 0.6, 9.7 %; RFT 5.7, 3.3, 3.8 %; MJWarp 40.5, 41.7, 40.9 %.
  The ankle roll torque reaches that value in at most 1 % of the samples of any policy.
- *Control (a): a torque cap on the hip pitch joints instead of the knees.* MPM `aga5ovkp`, +30°, hip pitch
  torque limit at the same shares of its configured 88 N m as the knee caps: 0.85 (75 N m), 0.75 (66 N m),
  0.64 (56 N m), 0.50 (44 N m); knee and every other limit as configured. 40 trials per step (seeds 41 to 48,
  5 starts each, the starts of the knee runs), beach sand, 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, default bed setting, footprint
  tracker on. The uncapped reference is the merged default run (40 of 40). New option
  `eval_walk.py --hip_pitch_torque_limit`, set before the simulation is built and read back from the
  simulation and from the solver's joint force range in every run, as for the knee.
- *Reading of (a), fixed now.* The cap has to bind as in the knee test (torque at or above 95 % of the cap in
  at least 10 % of the samples on the slope); a step that does not bind tests nothing. A step "fails" if MPM
  reaches at most 32 of 40 goals (Fisher's exact test against the uncapped 40 of 40, p below 0.01) and "holds"
  if it reaches at least 37 of 40; 33 to 36 is undecided. For comparison, the knee caps at the same four
  shares gave 28, 13, 2 and 0 of 40, so the knee fails from 0.85 on. *"Specific to the knee, at these
  levels"* if every binding hip pitch step holds and the steps 0.64 and 0.50 bind. *"Not specific to the knee:
  the policy needs its hip pitch torque as well"* if a binding hip pitch step fails; the highest failing share
  is then put beside the knee's 0.85, and the kinds of failure beside the knee's forward falls. Anything else
  is reported step by step without a summary word. Reported with the verdict: goals and failure kinds per
  step, the share of the uncapped policy's samples above each cap for both joints (the numbers above), since
  an equal share of the limit is not an equal intervention, and the sand motion under the sole per stance.
- *What (a) would and would not mean.* A cap on one joint also changes what the policy does with the others.
  If the hip pitch steps hold, the dependence on torque found in the knee test belongs to the knee and not to
  any strong leg joint; if they fail, the MPM policy as trained needs its leg torques at these levels in
  general, and the knee test says nothing special about the knee. Neither outcome compares the policies.
- *Control (b): the three policies with the ankle torque limit of the description files.* MPM `aga5ovkp`, RFT
  `6t0h2ud8`, MJWarp `3qymp37c`; torque limit of the ankle pitch and ankle roll joints 35 N m instead of 50 N m,
  knee and every other limit as configured (knee 139 N m); +20°, +25°, +30°, 40 trials per policy and slope
  (same seeds and starts), same sand, bed, speed, bed setting and tracker. New option
  `eval_walk.py --ankle_torque_limit`, read back in every run. Reference cells at 50 N m: the merged default
  runs (goals of 40 at +20°, +25°, +30°: MPM 40, 40, 40; RFT 40, 39, 13; MJWarp 0, 0, 0).
- *Reading of (b), fixed now.* Per policy and slope: the ankle limit "changes the outcome" if the goals at
  35 N m differ from those at 50 N m with Fisher's exact test p below 0.01, otherwise "no change shown";
  velocity error over the trials that reach the goal is given beside it. MPM against RFT at 35 N m, per slope:
  the reading of the entry of 02:54 (success by Fisher's exact test at 0.01; velocity error where both reach
  at least 10 goals, 0.05 m/s and 2 pooled standard deviations). *"The comparison stands with the description
  files' ankle limit"* if the three success verdicts are those at 50 N m (not separated at +20° and +25°, MPM
  better at +30°); otherwise the slopes that changed are named. Whether the limit binds is reported per cell
  (share of the samples on the slope with an ankle torque at or above 95 % of 35 N m). MJWarp is tabulated; it
  reaches no goal from +20° at 50 N m, so only a rise above 0 would be remarked.
- *Stated with (b) whichever way it comes out:* no policy was trained with the 35 N m limit; the result says
  how these three policies would do with it, not which training would be better.
- *Runs.* OSMO pool `isaac-lab-l40-07`, L40; (a) one workflow per seed and step (32), (b) one workflow per
  seed with the three slopes and three policies (8); submitted in waves of at most 16 workflows, after the
  lead confirms that this entry is committed. Local check before this entry, level ground, one trial, 60
  steps, hip pitch 44 N m and ankles 35 N m: the articulation data and the solver's joint force range read
  44 N m for the two hip pitch joints and 35 N m for the four ankle joints. That file is not a result.
- *Added 2026-11-25, 03:11, after check 8 (wording of the knee entries above, which are left as written).* (1) "The
  real G1's published maximum knee torque" and "about the real knee's torque" mean the 90 N m (G1) and 120 N m
  (G1 EDU) of Unitree's product page; its description files (URDF, MJCF) list 139 N m for the knee, the value
  simulated, and 35 N m for the ankles, where the simulation uses 50 N m. (2) "The largest knee torque in each
  cell equals the cap" is true by construction, since the recorded torque is clipped with the same limit; the
  evidence that the cap acts is the solver's joint force range read back in every run and the response of the
  goals. (3) "0.64 (89 N m, the 95th percentile of what RFT uses at +30° in trials that reach the goal)" is the
  value in the windows matched to RFT's falls of the pre-fall follow-up; over all samples on the slope of RFT's
  trials that reach the goal the 95th percentile is 0.49 to 0.59 of the limit (both knees pooled, and the
  larger knee per sample), so the step is at the upper end of RFT's use. It is RFT's use at +30° only (36 to
  39 N m at +25°, 32 to 34 N m at +20°). (4) The verdict "necessary" of the rule is a statement about the MPM
  policy; it does not say that the knee push is why MPM succeeds where RFT fails.
- *Added 2026-11-25, 04:34, after the runs (what was done; the rules of 02:54 and 03:05 are unchanged).* 56 workflows
  on OSMO pool `isaac-lab-l40-07` (L40), all completed at the first attempt, never more than 16 queued:

- *Added 2026-11-25, 04:44, after check 9 (corrections to the notes above, which are left as written).* (1) The note

**Added 2026-11-25, 17:33, before computing: is the RFT policy less steady than the MPM policy? (asked by Junnosuke
after the videos: "3D RFT policy looks way more unstable than MPM. Is there any way we can quantify this?")**
No new runs: the rollouts of the final rerun. This is a description of how steadily the two policies walk; it
is not a test of why RFT falls.

- *What I have already seen, so this entry is not blind for it.* The whole-body comparison (entry of 01:23)
  already computed some of the measures below, and I know their outcome on the slopes: the spread of the pelvis
  roll and the RMS pelvis roll rate separate the policies with MPM the larger; the RMS pelvis pitch rate, yaw
  rate, velocity normal to the slope and action rate do not separate; the ranges of the waist and shoulder
  joints separate with RFT the larger. I have watched the clips. The measures marked "new" have not been
  computed.
- *Data and window.* As in the whole-body comparison: slopes +20°, +25°, +30° on beach sand, 40 trials per
  policy and slope, bed settings T (start on the settled surface) and W (12 s settling, sand awake), the
  default setting reported beside them; the five sands of the material ladder on level ground, 10 trials per
  cell, settings T and W. On a slope the window starts 0.3 m past the start of the slope, on level ground at
  1.5 s; a trial that does not reach the goal is cut 0.5 s before its end; a trial needs three gait cycles.
  RFT and MPM carry the reading; MJWarp is tabulated where it has trials.
- *Measures, per trial.* (1) RMS angular velocity of the pelvis about the roll, pitch and yaw axes of the
  heading frame, and of the three combined [deg/s]. (2) New: RMS angular velocity of the torso (pelvis
  angular velocity plus the three waist joints), three axes combined [deg/s]. (3) New: RMS linear acceleration
  of the pelvis, from the recorded velocity at 50 Hz, three axes combined [m/s²]. (4) Standard deviation of
  the pelvis pitch (new) and roll about their trial means [deg]. (5) New: step-to-step variability, the
  coefficient of variation over the steps of a trial of step time, step length along the slope and step
  width [%]. (6) New: lateral sway, the standard deviation of the pelvis position across the walking direction
  after removing a straight line over the window [cm]. (7) New: RMS joint velocity of the 14 arm joints and of
  the 3 waist joints [rad/s]. (8) RMS change of the action per control step, leg joints and all joints (the
  second is new). (9) New: position of the pelvis relative to the centre of the supporting sole during single
  support, along and across the walking direction: mean and standard deviation over the samples of a trial
  [cm] (the pelvis stands in for the centre of mass, which is not recorded). (10) The spread of the forward
  speed about its trial mean [m/s], from the whole-body comparison.
- *Two summary numbers, fixed now.* (A) trunk motion: measure (2). (B) step irregularity: the coefficient of
  variation of the step time. They are what a viewer would call unsteady: a trunk that swings and steps that
  are not alike.
- *Reading, fixed now.* A measure "separates" the policies on the slopes (on the sands) if RFT and MPM differ
  by at least two pooled standard deviations over trials, with the same sign, in T and in W, in each of the
  three slopes (five sands); the size is given as the ratio RFT over MPM per cell. "RFT walks less steadily
  than MPM on the slopes" is written only if (A) and (B) both separate with RFT the larger; if one separates,
  that one is named and the other is said not to; if neither, "not shown by these measures", and the measures
  that do separate are listed with their direction, whichever policy they favour. The same for the sands.
- *Relation to the falls, fixed now.* Reported for every measure that separates on the slopes, with the test
  of the entry of 01:23: among RFT's trials at +30°, over the first four gait cycles on the slope, area under
  the ROC curve of later falling against reaching the goal at least 0.70 pooled over the three settings with
  the task's sleeping and above 0.5 in at least two of them. A measure that does not pass is described as a
  difference in how the policies move, not as a reason for the falls.

**Added 2026-11-25, 17:48, before computing the trend: does RFT's unsteadiness build up with the slope, and faster
than MPM's? (asked by Junnosuke after the steadiness result)** No new runs. The question is about a trend over
the slopes; the data can show a trend, not its cause.

- *What I have already seen, so this entry is not blind for it.* The steadiness result above gave the values at
  +20°, +25°, +30° in the settings T, W and default, and on level beach sand in T: the torso's angular velocity
  rises with the slope for both policies (RFT 151, 159, 175 deg/s, MPM 101, 109, 121 deg/s in T; level 120 and
  76) and their ratio is about the same on every slope (1.4 to 1.6). So I expect two rising curves with a
  roughly constant ratio. Not seen: the cells at +5°, +10°, +15° and downhill, the other measures as curves,
  and any test within RFT's trials other than the one for the first four gait cycles at +30°.
- *Data.* Beach sand, 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s. Default bed setting: level and +5° to +30° in 5° steps, and −5° to
  −30° for reference; 10 trials per policy below +15° and downhill (seeds 41, 42), 40 trials from +15° (seeds 41
  to 48). Settings T and W: level (the beach-sand cell of the material ladder, 10 trials) and +15°, +20°, +25°,
  +30° (40 trials); there are no runs at +5° and +10° in T and W. The default setting carries the trend, T and
  W are the check. Window and trial rules as in the steadiness entry.
- *Measures against slope, per policy (RFT, MPM; MJWarp where at least three of its trials have a window).*
  The two summary numbers, (A) torso angular velocity (RMS) and (B) coefficient of variation of the step time;
  the spread of the forward speed; the lateral sway of the pelvis; the RMS velocity of the waist joints and of
  the arm joints. On the same axis, without a rule: goals reached and the velocity error of the trials that
  reach the goal.
- *(a) Does a measure rise with the slope for RFT?* Rank correlation of the cell means with the slope over the
  seven uphill cells of the default setting (level to +30°). "Rises" if it is at least 0.9 and the cell at +30°
  is above the level cell by at least two pooled standard deviations over trials. The same number is given for
  MPM, which is question (d).
- *(b) Does the gap between RFT and MPM grow?* Per slope the difference RFT minus MPM of the cell means and
  their ratio. Trend: the slope of a straight line through the differences against the slope angle, with a 95 %
  interval from resampling the trials inside every cell 2000 times; the same for the logarithm of the ratio.
  "The gap grows" if the interval of the difference's trend lies above zero in the default setting and the
  trend has the same sign in T and in W; "parallel" if the interval contains zero. If the difference grows and
  the ratio does not, that is said as "both rise in proportion".
- *(c) Do RFT's trials with more of a measure fall more often?* The test of the entry of 01:23, for each of the
  six measures: among RFT's trials at +30°, over the first four gait cycles on the slope, area under the ROC
  curve of later falling against reaching the goal at least 0.70 pooled over the three settings with the
  task's sleeping and above 0.5 in at least two of them. At +25° RFT fails in 2 of 160 trials over the four
  settings, so nothing can be tested there, and that is said.
- *(d) MPM.* (a) for the MPM policy, and for each measure the slope at which RFT's curve (straight lines
  between its cells) has the value that MPM has at +30°.
- *Reading, fixed now.* "RFT's unsteadiness builds up with the slope" is written if (a) holds for RFT for both
  summary numbers and (b) gives "the gap grows" for both; if that holds for one of the two, it is written for
  that one and the other is named as not rising or not growing. "It leads to RFT's failure" is written only if
  (c) passes for a measure. A constant gap is reported as "a constant difference between the two policies, not
  something that builds up toward the failure". Words about a cause (a mismatch of the model, compensation)
  are not used.

**Added 2026-11-25, 18:01, before any run: does RFT become unstable when it climbs for longer? (asked by Junnosuke:
"if you run policy longer on a longer slope, does RFT become unstable at the end? You can try 20 s at least")**
The slope trials so far end at the goal line after about 8 s, of which about 5 s are on the slope.

- *Runs.* RFT `6t0h2ud8` and MPM `aga5ovkp` on beach sand, 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, footprint tracker on. The same
  slope tile as before, made larger (56 m instead of 16 m, so 26 m of slope ahead of the robot instead of 7 m),
  the goal line moved out of reach (25 m past the start of the slope) and the horizon set to 23 s, so that a
  trial that stays up ends by time after at least 20 s on the slope. Everything else as in the merged slope
  runs: default bed setting (start with the drop, 2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping) at +20°, +25°, +30°,
  and setting W (12 s settling, sand kept awake) at +25° and +30°; 40 trials per cell (seeds 41 to 48, five
  robots per run, each on its own tile and its own sand). MJWarp is not run (it falls within 4 s on these
  slopes). Before the runs a local check has to show that the particle patch and the rigid base 0.50 m below
  the surface follow the robot to the end of a 23 s trial; it is reported with the runs.
- *Time axis.* Time on the slope, from the moment the pelvis is 0.3 m past the start of the slope, in 2 s bins
  up to 20 s. A fall is a termination of the task (pelvis tilted past 45° or too low); a trial that leaves
  its lane is counted as lost to observation at that time, not as a fall.
- *Measured per bin, over the trials still up:* share of trials still up (survival curve); forward speed, mean
  and spread inside the bin; trunk motion (RMS angular velocity of the torso); pelvis pitch; share of the
  samples with a knee at or above 95 % of its torque limit; sand motion under the sole per stance; foot
  sinkage at mid-stance. For the trials that fall: the time of the fall.
- *Reading for the fall rate, fixed now.* Falls per robot-second at risk in the first 10 s on the slope against
  the second 10 s. With n falls in all, the number in the second half is compared with a binomial
  distribution whose share is the second half's share of the robot-seconds at risk (two-sided). "The fall
  rate rises with time" if the second half's rate is the higher and p is below 0.01; "falls early" if it is
  the lower and p is below 0.01; otherwise "a constant fall rate is not rejected". Fewer than 5 falls in a
  cell: no statement about the rate.
- *Reading for drift, fixed now.* For the trials that are up for the whole 20 s: the value over 14 to 20 s
  minus the value over 2 to 8 s (the first 2 s on the slope are the transition from level ground), per trial.
  A measure "drifts" for a policy if the mean of these differences is at least one standard deviation over
  trials of the 2 to 8 s value, upward for the measures of unsteadiness (forward-speed spread, trunk motion).
  As a check that does not depend on surviving, for every trial with at least 6 s on the slope the slope of a
  straight line through its 2 s bins (the last second before a fall left out) is given per 10 s.
- *Statements.* "RFT becomes unstable with time on the slope" is written for a slope and bed setting if RFT's
  fall rate rises with time, or if its forward-speed spread or trunk motion drifts while MPM's does not.
  "No drift" is written if a constant fall rate is not rejected and neither measure drifts. Anything else is
  described as found.
- *+20° and +25°, fixed now.* RFT reaches the 8 s goal there in 39 to 40 of 40. Reported: the share still up
  after 8 s on the slope and after 20 s. "Still stands after 20 s" if at least 37 of 40 are up at 20 s;
  "does not keep climbing: the earlier result was a statement about 8 s" if at most 32 of 40 are up at 20 s
  and that differs from the share up at 8 s in the same runs (Fisher's exact test, p below 0.01); 33 to 36 is
  undecided.
- *What it would and would not mean.* A fall rate that is constant in time means that falls arrive at a steady
  rate and a longer climb only gives them more time; a rising rate or a drift would mean that something
  builds up. Neither says why.

**Added 2026-11-25, 19:20, before any run: the slope tile is a valley, the long runs move to a planar ramp, and the
merged slope runs get a planar counterpart (amendment to the entry of 18:01, which is left as written).**

- *What was found in the local check of the long tile.* The slope tile of all slope runs so far is the training
  sub-terrain, Isaac Lab's pyramid slope, whose height is the product of two ramps (`hf_raw = height_max * xx *
  yy`). The walking test sends the robot from the platform along the tile's axis, the crease of that surface:
  uphill the robot climbs the floor of a V-shaped valley, the ground rising to both sides; downhill it walks
  along a ridge. Measured from the surface heights recorded under the feet in the scored runs (16 m tile, MPM):
  at +30° the slope along the walk is 29.7°, 29.2° and 28.8° at 3, 5 and 6.5 m from the tile centre, and the
  slope across, under each foot and rising outward, is 19 to 20°, 11° and 6° (the formula gives 19.8°, 12.2°,
  6.2°); at +20° the slope across is 12.6°, 7.4° and 3.6°.
- *Why the design of 18:01 is withdrawn.* Making the tile larger changes the valley: on a 56 m tile the side
  slopes are 27° at 3 m and 20° at 10 m. In the local check 3 of 3 MPM robots fell there (5.6, 8.8, 15.2 s,
  forward, sand and its depth in place), while the MPM policy has not fallen once on the 16 m tile. The 56 m
  tile is a different, harder terrain and is not used. These local trials are not results.
- *New terrain: a planar ramp with a level approach* (`scripts/benchmarks/t3/ramp_terrain.py`, new;
  `eval_walk.py --terrain ramp --approach 1.87`). A level strip on which the robot starts, then a plane that
  rises along the walk with no slope across; the start of the slope is 1.87 m ahead of the start, as on the
  16 m tile. Checked locally at +30°: the recorded surface has 30.0° along and 0.00° across; three MPM
  robots reach the goal, two of three RFT robots do. A start on the ramp itself was tried and dropped: three of
  three RFT robots fall within 2 s of a standing start on a 30° ramp, which would test the start and not the
  climb. So, differently from what I proposed, the robot starts on the level strip in both items below.
- *Item A, the planar counterpart of the merged slope runs.* MJWarp `3qymp37c`, RFT `6t0h2ud8`, MPM `aga5ovkp`
  on the planar ramp at +20°, +25°, +30°; everything else as in the merged runs of the default bed setting:
  beach sand, 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, goal line 5 m past the start of the slope, horizon 13 s, lane half-width
  1.5 m, footprint tracker on, 40 trials per cell (seeds 41 to 48, five robots per run, each on its own lane
  and its own sand). MJWarp alone also at +10° and +15°, to see where it stops climbing.
- *Reading of A, fixed now.* Per policy and slope: goals of 40, kinds of failure, velocity error of the trials
  that reach the goal, beside the valley's numbers (default setting, goals at +20°, +25°, +30°: MJWarp 0, 0, 0;

  reach 10 goals). *"The slope result holds on a planar slope"* if the three success verdicts are the valley's
  (not separated at +20° and +25°, MPM better at +30°); otherwise the slopes that differ are named. Per policy
  and slope, *"the valley changes the outcome"* if the goals on the planar ramp differ from the valley's with
  Fisher's exact test p below 0.01; otherwise "no change shown". MJWarp: the steepest slope with at least 37
  of 40 goals and the shallowest with at most 3, beside the valley's (10 of 10 at level, 9 of 10 at +5°, 4 of
  10 at +10°, 0 of 40 from +15°).

  slope 25 m long, horizon 23 s, lanes 12 m wide (a robot is lost to observation 5.5 m to the side), the
  particle store sized for the strip a robot walks; default setting at +20°, +25°, +30° and setting W at +25°
  and +30°; 40 trials per cell (seeds 41 to 50, four robots per run, since a robot takes about 9 GB of GPU

- *What this does not change.* The merged slope numbers stay as measured; what changes is their description:
