**What to know before using the column.**

- *Cells that rest on few or failing trials.* MJWarp reaches no goal at +20° and +30° and on (1000, 0.70) and
  (640, 0.73), so those cells are dashes under the table's convention. Its foot does touch the sand there: over
  all trials up to the fall the value is 1.10 m/s at +20° (10 failing trials, 94 stances), 1.20 m/s at +30°
  (10, 76), 1.42 m/s on (1000, 0.70) (10, 38) and 1.48 m/s on (640, 0.73) (10, 24); these are a falling robot's
  feet and should not be read as stance. MJWarp at +10° rests on 4 trials (all 10 trials: 0.94 m/s), on
  (682, 1.07) on 9. RFT at +30° rests on 3 trials (all 10 trials: 0.66 m/s).
- *The value depends on the bed setting much more than SR and e_v do.* In setting W (12 s settling, sand kept
  awake) the stance foot velocity on the sands is 0.3 to 1.0 times the value in T (table below); only the two
  cells on dense sand for RFT and MPM agree within 0.05 m/s. The order between the policies is the same in
  both: MJWarp highest on every sand where it walks, RFT and MPM close on the firm sands, MPM above RFT on the
  two softest.
- *SR and e_v disagree between T and W* for MJWarp on (682, 1.07) (90 % and 0.48 m/s in T, 100 % and 0.06 m/s
  in W) and on (1000, 0.70) (0 % in T, 100 % and 0.39 m/s in W). RFT at +30° is 30 % here (3 of 10); over the
  four bed preparations with 40 trials it is 1 to 18 of 40.
- *Higher is not simply worse.* On the soft sands MPM's stance foot velocity is above RFT's (0.40 against
  0.23, 0.53 against 0.41, 0.85 against 0.64 m/s in T) while its e_v is lower. A foot counts as in stance from
  20 N, which includes landing, lift-off and a lightly loaded toe (fifth check), and the value is read from
  the force at the last physics step of each control step. I would give the column no better/worse arrow.
- *Step time of the material rows.* The current table's material rows use the step time from touchdown to
  touchdown of opposite feet (`material_behaviour.json`), its slope rows the count from the ankle height. The
  rows here use the second for all; the material rows differ from the current table by at most 0.02 s
  ((682, 1.07): 0.22 / 0.24 / 0.27 against 0.20 / 0.24 / 0.26). The CSV has both.
- *Hard ground.* 0.01 m/s for every policy: the foot does not move while it is loaded.

### Paper figures as a pair of 1 by 3 plots (2026-11-24, stance foot velocity unchecked)

Requested by Junnosuke through the lead: the slope and the material figure of the paper with the same three
panels in one row. New files in the paper's `figures/`, nothing replaced, no `.tex` touched:
`t3_slope_1x3_final.pdf` and `t3_material_1x3_final.pdf`. Both 3.45 by 1.42 in (single column), same panel
rectangles, fonts, policy colours, y-axis ranges and legend place (lower left of the first panel).

- *Panels, left to right:* success rate [%]; velocity tracking error [m/s]; stance foot velocity [m/s]. The
  error and the stance foot velocity are means over the trials that reach the goal, band one standard
  deviation; where a policy has no such trial it has no point (as in the earlier figures, which carry no extra
  mark either; the success rate panel shows the 0 %).
- *Slope figure:* x = slope angle, −30° to +30° in 5° steps, beach sand (1800 kg/m³, 0.80), 0.50 m bed, 10
  trials per policy and slope. Bed setting: the slope sweep's default (the robot is dropped onto the settled
  surface; 2.5 s settling; the task's sleeping). Values from `logs/t3/final_1/d50/geometry/geometry.json`, the
  data of `t3_slope_full_final3.pdf`; grey marks the slopes seen in training.
- *Material figure:* x = the six grounds from stiff to soft, labelled rigid, (3000, 0.90), (1800, 0.80),
  (682, 1.07), (1000, 0.70), (640, 0.73) as (density [kg/m³], friction); level ground, 0.50 m bed, 10 trials per
  policy and ground. Bed setting: T (start on the settled surface, 2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping); hard
  ground is rigid contact. Values from the table block above (`logs/t3/final_1/walk_table/t3_walk_table.json`).
  In this setting the two figures differ in the start (drop against settled surface), so their common ground,
  level beach sand, is not the same run: e_v 0.10 / 0.10 / 0.06 against 0.10 / 0.09 / 0.06 m/s, stance foot
  velocity 0.53 / 0.12 / 0.13 against 0.51 / 0.12 / 0.14 m/s.
- *Stance foot velocity, one sentence for the caption:* "Stance foot velocity is the mean horizontal speed of
  the sole centre over the control samples in which the foot carries more than 20 N, averaged over the trials
  that reach the goal." It is the quantity of the "contact foot velocity" panel of the earlier slope figure,
  the same definition in both figures; it is not independently checked, and it is not the bearing-point speed
  of the fifth check.
- *What a reader should not take from the third panel:* the value depends on the bed setting (in setting W it
  is 0.3 to 1.0 times the T value on the sands) and higher is not simply worse (on the soft sands MPM's is
  above RFT's while its error is lower).
- *On the page:* both shown beside the current figures of the slope and the material slide, labelled
  "Proposed paper figure", outside the figure numbering so the existing numbers stay.
- Code: `paper_figures.py --pair logs/t3/final_1/d50/geometry/geometry.json
  logs/t3/final_1/walk_table/t3_walk_table.json --suffix _final` (`pair_figure`, `slope_1x3`, `material_1x3`).
- *Second version, same afternoon:* the x label of the slope figure is "Terrain slope [deg]" (the note "< 0:
  downhill" is gone from the axis and has to be in the caption), and the third panel's title has 1 pt more
  room on the right. Same size and data. Files `t3_slope_1x3_final2.pdf` and `t3_material_1x3_final2.pdf`; the
  sole-angle figure has the same x label in `t3_sole_closeup_final4.pdf`. The earlier files are left in place.
- *Merged version (evening of 2026-11-24):* `t3_walk_2x3_final.pdf`, 3.45 by 2.38 in, single column: the slope
  row on top and the material row below, with the same panels, lines, bands, colours and y-ranges as the two
  1 by 3 files (which rendered identically before and after the code was restructured for it). Column titles
  once above the top row, one legend in the first panel, "Terrain slope [deg]" under the top row and "Ground
  material (ρ [kg/m³], μ)" under the bottom row. The two separate figures take 2.84 in together. Bed settings
  as stated above: top row default start with the drop, bottom row setting T. On the page it is shown first,
  before the two 1 by 3 figures (`site_slides.py --paper-merged`).

### Alternatives of the paper's material figure: density axis, and 3D (2026-11-24, unchecked)

Asked by Junnosuke through the lead: could the material figure be a 3D plot, or is 2D easier to read? Two
alternatives were drawn beside `t3_material_1x3_final2.pdf`, as new files in the paper's `figures/`; nothing
was replaced, no new runs, and they have not been handed to the paper session.

- *Data for both:* the density-friction grid on level ground, 0.50 m bed, setting T (start on the settled
  surface, 2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping), each sand settled as itself, 5 trials per sand and policy
  (`logs/t3/final_1/gridT`). Sands whose bed holds: friction 0.7 and 0.9 in T and in W, friction 0.5 in T only.
  The stance foot velocity does exist for the grid runs (`foot_slip_m_s` in `gridT/report/trials.csv`, the same
  definition as in the 1 by 3 pair); it is not independently checked.
- *A, `t3_material_1x3_density.pdf`* (3.45 by 1.42 in, the pair's panel rectangles, fonts and colours): success
  rate, velocity error and stance foot velocity against bulk density on a log axis, stiff on the left as in the
  material figure, hard ground as a separate marker left of 3000 kg/m³ (from the ladder's rigid row). One line
  per policy for friction 0.9 (solid, filled markers) and friction 0.7 (dashed, hollow markers): 14 sands, 7
  densities from 250 to 3000 kg/m³. Each point is 5 trials for the success rate; error and stance foot velocity
  are over the trials that reach the goal, which is 5 everywhere except MJWarp at 1000 kg/m³ with friction 0.9
  (3 trials) and at 1500 kg/m³ with friction 0.7 (1 trial), and it has no point at 500 kg/m³ (friction 0.9) and
  1000 kg/m³ (friction 0.7), where it reaches no goal. The y-ranges of the two right panels are wider than in
  the pair (0 to 0.8 m/s and 0 to 1.6 m/s instead of 0 to 0.6 and 0 to 1.05), because the grid reaches softer
  sands than the six grounds.
- *B, `t3_material_1x3_3d.pdf`* (3.45 by 1.42 in): the same three quantities as 3D surfaces over density and
  friction in the style of the earlier mesh figure, sands whose bed holds in T (friction 0.5, 0.7, 0.9; 21
  sands), crosses on the floor where the bed collapses.
- *What the grid shows that the six grounds do not:* MJWarp's success is not monotonic in density (goals of 5
  at 3000 … 250 kg/m³: friction 0.9: 5, 5, 5, 5, 3, 0, 5; friction 0.7: 5, 5, 5, 1, 0, 5, 5); RFT and MPM reach
  5 of 5 on all 14 sands; RFT's error lies above MPM's on every sand; the stance foot velocity rises steeply
  below 1000 kg/m³ for every policy.
- *My view on readability at 3.45 in:* A is readable but busier than the six-ground figure (six lines per
  panel); the hollow dashed lines of friction 0.7 lie close to the solid ones except for MJWarp. B is not
  readable at this width: three overlapping translucent surfaces per panel, tick labels of 4 pt, and with
  three friction values the surface is a strip. The six-ground figure remains the clearest; A is the option if
  a continuous axis is wanted.
- Code: `paper_figures.py --material_alt logs/t3/final_1/gridT logs/t3/final_1/gridT/collapse.json
  logs/t3/final_1/walk_table/t3_walk_table.json` (`material_density_1x3`, `material_3d`); on the page both are
  shown after the proposed material figure as "Alternative A" and "Alternative B" (`site_slides.py
  --material-alt`).

## P1-1 terrain geometry, explanation: sand under the foot on slopes (2026-11-26)

Status: measured in this session, unchecked. Definitions: the block above ("definitions fixed before running"). Slides: [site/T3/index.html](../site/T3/index.html). Data: `logs/t3/final_1/d50/flow_report/slope_flow.json`.

<b>Final rerun, 2026-11-24; checked the same day (fifth check: every number reproduced; the reading of the foot's motion against the sand was not supported and is withdrawn below).</b> MJWarp <code>3qymp37c</code>, RFT <code>6t0h2ud8</code> and MPM <code>aga5ovkp</code> at their final checkpoints (iteration 19,999), all three in every workflow and all workflows from one code bundle. Beach sand (density 1800 kg/m³, friction 0.80, no cohesion), 1.0 m/s command, slopes −30° to +30° in 5° steps. This slide is built from the 0.50 m bed, the same bed as the headline sweep: 10 trials per policy and slope (seeds 41 and 42, 5 trials each, because 10 trials at this depth do not fit in the memory of one L40). The stance definition, the close-up rule, the failure rule and the depth test are in the task record ("definitions fixed before running"); the check could not verify that they were written before the runs. Every quantity is over all trials that took at least three stances on the slope, failed trials included up to the fall.

<b>Why the 0.50 m bed.</b> On the 0.35 m bed the loaded feet come within 2 cm of the rigid base at +30° (in 36 of 40 MPM trials and 33 of 40 RFT trials) and for MJWarp from +20°. On the 0.50 m bed they do not: one trial of 120 at +30° (MPM, 1.9 cm) and none of RFT. Between 0.35 and 0.50 m no RFT cell and one MPM cell (−30°, velocity error 0.36 to 0.28 m/s) changed by more than the tolerance fixed beforehand; 12 MJWarp cells changed, always for the worse on the deeper bed. Tables: block "Bed depth" of the geometry slide.

<b>In one paragraph.</b> H1 holds: the sand under a loaded foot moves downhill by an amount that grows with the slope, under every policy. H2 holds against MJWarp at every uphill slope from +10° and against RFT at +30° only; at +25° MPM and RFT are not separated (40 against 39 of 40 trials on this bed). Under MPM's sole the sand moves 20 to 40 % less than under RFT's from +15° to +30°, in every seed and in all four bed settings; no measured behaviour explains it. Downhill MPM is not better: every policy reaches the goal and MPM's velocity error is the largest.

<b>Supported.</b> After the foot has dug in, the sand that lay within 3 cm under the sole moves downhill during the rest of the stance by about 0 cm on level ground (MJWarp's foot drags it 1.2 cm forward), then at +10°, +20° and +30° by 2.0, 5.0 and 10.8 cm under MJWarp, 1.4, 3.6 and 6.6 cm under RFT and 1.0, 2.2 and 5.2 cm under MPM. Downhill it is 2.0–3.1 cm at −10°, 4.6–6.3 cm at −20° and 10.7–12.1 cm at −30° (range over the policies). The rank correlation between slope angle and this motion over trials is above 0.9 for every policy, uphill and downhill (0.96, 0.99 and 0.99 uphill with tied ranks averaged, as the check recomputed; the 0.92, 0.97 and 0.97 quoted earlier came from a ranking that did not average ties).

<b>Which trials are behind these values.</b> Failed trials are included up to the fall, so MJWarp's uphill values come from the stances it took before falling: 6 trials at +10° (4 of them reach the goal) and at +20° (none does), 2 trials and 6 stances at +30°, and no value at +25°. Its point at +30° in the figures (sand motion 10.8 cm, sole 70° toes-down, 1.03 m/s) rests on those 6 stances. RFT's values at +30° include its 7 failing trials (successful trials only: 5.9 instead of 6.6 cm). The sand motion is the median over the particles within 3 cm under the sole, not an average over the sole. +10° was run in the default setting only; +15° to +30° also with the start on the settled surface, with 6 s settling and with the sand kept awake (setting W), where every sand motion is about a third lower (2.6 against 1.5 cm for RFT and MPM at +20°).

Unlike on the 0.25 m bed of the first pass, the policies do not move the sand equally: uphill the order is MJWarp, RFT, MPM at every slope. The earlier statement that the sand motion is "the same for every policy" was a result of the shallow bed, where the feet stood near the base, and is withdrawn.

The foot is what moves the sand. Around each footprint 21–30 L of sand move more than 1 cm per stance on level ground and 32–35 L at +30°. In the control patch 0.6 m to the outer side of the foot nothing moves between −10° and +25° (0.05 L at most); at −30° 3.7–4.1 L move there, so on the steepest downhill slope the bed itself is close to sliding.

<b>Supported uphill against MJWarp, and against RFT at +30° only.</b> Goals of 10 on the 0.50 m bed at +10°, +15°, +20°, +25°, +30°: MJWarp 4, 0, 0, 0, 0; RFT 10, 10, 10, 10, 3; MPM 10, 10, 10, 10, 10. With 40 trials per cell (seeds 41 to 48): MJWarp 0 of 40 from +15°; RFT 40, 40, 39 and 13 of 40 at +15°, +20°, +25° and +30°; MPM 40 of 40 at each. Fisher's exact test, MPM against RFT: p = 1 at +25° (not separated), p = 2e-11 at +30° (separated). The same holds on the 0.35 m bed (37 against 40 at +25°, p = 0.24; 10 against 40 at +30°).

<b>Velocity error (all trials).</b> Uphill MPM tracks best: 0.06–0.07 m/s up to +20° and 0.13 m/s at +30°, against 0.10–0.13 and 0.22 m/s for RFT. Downhill MPM tracks worst at the steep end: 0.28, 0.20 and 0.14 m/s at −30°, −25° and −20°, against 0.22, 0.14 and 0.10 m/s for RFT and 0.23, 0.15 and 0.10 m/s for MJWarp. Every policy reaches the goal on every downhill slope.

<b>Under MPM's sole the sand moves less than under RFT's; no measured behaviour explains it.</b> Sand under the sole per stance, RFT against MPM: 3.6 against 2.2 cm at +20° and 4.5 against 3.4 cm at +25° (10 trials); on 40 trials 2.5 against 1.6, 3.5 against 2.2, 4.5 against 3.3 and 6.4 against 5.1 cm at +15°, +20°, +25° and +30°. That is 20 to 40 % less, in every one of the 8 seeds and in all four bed settings (as recomputed by the fifth check). <b>Withdrawn:</b> the earlier reading "MPM's foot does not move less, the sand under it does", with RFT's foot carried by the sand and MPM's foot sliding 3.1–3.5 cm over it. The check found four things against it. (i) The whole-stance motion of the bearing point is the sum of its motion while digging in, the sand motion and a remainder that was called slide: at +20° RFT 0.7 + 3.6 + 0.3 = 4.5 cm, MPM −1.5 + 2.2 + 3.4 = 4.2 cm. The two totals are close only because MPM's foot still moves uphill while it digs in; sand plus remainder is 5.7 cm for MPM, more than its total, and with the sand kept awake the totals differ (3.9 against 1.3 cm). (ii) The bearing point is the toe, and MPM's remainder is its toe moving during a long, lightly loaded end of stance: while the foot carries at least half the body weight MPM's bearing point moves 2.4 cm against RFT's 4.7 cm at +20°. (iii) Measured at the sole centre neither foot slides downhill (−1.9 and −1.2 cm at +20°). (iv) With the sand kept awake the contrast vanishes at +15° (1.3 against 1.4 cm). The table keeps the three terms as measured values; no conclusion is drawn from them, and none from the two whole-stance values being close.

<b>How MJWarp fails.</b> Its 47 failed uphill trials on this bed are 25 collapses (pelvis too low), 19 falls forward, 2 falls sideways and 1 lane exit. "Leaves the lane sideways", the main failure on the 0.25 m bed, was a result of standing on the base there. RFT's 7 failures at +30° are 6 falls forward and 1 lane exit. This is the default setting; with the sand kept awake MJWarp's failures at +15° are mostly lane exits (15 of 17, from the check).

<b>How fast the bearing point of the sole moves.</b> From the first to the last sample in which the foot carries more than 20 N, the bearing point of MJWarp's sole covers 0.53–1.03 m/s on every slope; on level ground it is 0.54 m/s, and 0.29 m/s with the sand kept awake. For RFT and MPM it is 0.11–0.16 m/s on level ground and rises to 0.84 and 0.70 m/s at +30°. These values include touchdown and lift-off. While the foot carries at least half the body weight, the check found 0.25 m/s for MJWarp on level ground (0.10 m/s awake) and 0.08–0.10 m/s for RFT and MPM, and at +30° MPM's is higher than RFT's (0.82 against 0.62 m/s), the reverse of the whole-stance order. So no order between RFT and MPM at +30° is claimed, and "MJWarp's foot never comes to rest" is not claimed either. What sets MJWarp apart on every slope is the sole angle (next paragraph), not step timing or loading.

<b>Sole angle.</b> MJWarp holds the sole 24° toes-down already on level ground (15° with the sand kept awake, so this value depends on the bed setting), 44° at +10° (run in the default setting only) and 57° at +20° (37° below level; 56° to 58° in all four settings): it bears on the toe edge. RFT and MPM hold it within 3° of level on level ground and 6° and 8° below level at +20°. At +30° both are about 20° below level (51° and 48° toes-down to the slope).

<b>Step timing.</b> On level ground MJWarp steps fastest (step time 0.22 s, RFT 0.26 s, MPM 0.30 s) with the shortest steps. Uphill from +10° all three step in 0.19–0.24 s with 0.20–0.25 m steps, so stance duration, step length and step time do not separate the policies where the goals do. MJWarp's values there rest on 6, 7, 6, 0 and 2 trials at +10° to +30°.

<b>Loading.</b> Peak load per stance at +20°: 2.0 body weights (MJWarp), 1.1 (RFT), 1.9 (MPM). MPM loads the foot about as hard as MJWarp and still reaches the goal, so a softer touchdown is not what separates them. Peak loads were not part of the check. Uphill, load comes onto the foot within about one physics step (5 ms) for every policy.

At +20° the median trial of RFT and of MPM reached the goal; MJWarp's ended with the pelvis too low. MJWarp's loaded sole stands 57° toes-down to the slope with the toe buried; RFT and MPM hold it 26° and 28° toes-down, which is 6° and 8° below level on a 20° slope. In every row the sand downhill of the loaded foot has moved several centimetres downhill and the sand uphill of it has barely moved. The frames differ in stance phase because the frame is chosen by rule, 0.1 s after a foot first takes load.

<b>Rigid slopes (same slopes, no sand, same bundle, 10 trials).</b> Between −20° and +15° every policy reaches 10 of 10 on rigid ground. At the extremes each policy is best on the ground it trained on. At +30°: MJWarp 9 of 10 on rigid ground and 0 of 10 on sand; RFT 8 and 3; MPM 0 and 10. At −30°: MJWarp 9 and 10; RFT 7 and 10; MPM 5 (velocity error 0.70 m/s) and 10 (0.28 m/s). So MPM is not the better walker everywhere: it is the better walker on sand, and the worst on steep rigid ground.

<b>Sliding angle of the sand.</b> Unloaded beach sand has no sharp sliding angle in this simulation: it creeps, and by the 1 cm/s rule it slides at 35° (friction 0.80). All slopes of the sweep are below that, which fits the still control patch up to +25°.

<b>Limits.</b> One training seed and one checkpoint per policy. Ten trials per cell here (40 at +15° to +30°); the start states on the 0.50 m bed are not the same ten as on the other beds. MJWarp's results still depend on the bed depth: on every soft or steep cell it does worse on the deeper bed, so its numbers on a deeper bed could be worse still where it has not already failed.

Proposal for one single-column figure for P1. Top row: (a) goals reached and (b) velocity error against slope, −30° to +30°, three policies, 0.50 m bed. Middle row: (c) sand under the sole moving downhill per stance against slope (H1, and MPM's line lowest uphill) and (d) sole angle to the slope in mid-stance with the level-sole line (how MJWarp differs). Bottom row: the material ladder on level ground, velocity error and one behaviour quantity against the materials from hard ground to loose poppy seed.

What it would not show: the rigid-ground sweep (each policy best on its own ground), which deserves one sentence in the text, and the bed-depth dependence of MJWarp, which belongs in the limitations.

<!-- T3-box-test:start -->
## P2 object towing: material not shown on the slides, kept for later

Two tests were taken off `site/T3/index.html` and the published copy on 2026-11-23 at Junnosuke's request (the
P3 slide was too crowded and the confining channel will not be used in the paper). Nothing was deleted: data,
scripts, figures and videos are where they were, and the last page that showed them is kept as
`site/T3/p3_channel_not_shown_20261123.html`. The P3 slide now shows goal reaching with the tether-cut control,
the force figure and the short deformation result only.

### A. Controlled box test in a sand channel (2026-11-22)

Status: measured in this session, unchecked. The paper figure
`figures/t3_tow.pdf` still uses this test as its right panel; `figures/t3_tow_v2.pdf` is the version without it.

**Question.** Does the sand a box has displaced change the force on it?

**Setup.** A 15 kg cube (0.3 m, weight 147 N) on the T1 probe's fixed MPM bed, free to sink under its own
weight but held level, dragged at 0.5 m/s 4.8 m out and 4.8 m back through its own track. Bed 6.4 m long and
either 0.36 m wide (the sand cannot pass the cube) or 1.6 m wide. One run per bed.

**Result.** Force averaged over 1.5–2.5 m ("early") and 3.5–4.5 m ("late") of each pass.

**How the force was recorded (added 2026-11-24, unchecked).** The forces below are read at the last physics
step of each control step; probably 5 to 10 % low, not proven. This test is the T1 probe: the horizontal drive is
a PD position target (1e5 N/m, 4e3 N s/m) that jumps once per control step (1 cm every 20 ms at 0.5 m/s), and
the sand force is read once, after the last of the four physics steps. Estimate from the saved records: on the
way back the cube does not sink, so the mean vertical sand force must equal its weight (147 N) and 134 N was
recorded (0.91; on the way out 0.92–0.93 with the dashpot's share taken off); the horizontal drive force,
assuming the cube moves evenly within a control step, gives 543–756 N against 496–718 N recorded (0.91–0.95).
Without that assumption the records only bound the true mean between 0.4 and 2.5 times the recorded value. The
statements of this test are ratios between passes and beds; its absolute forces are not used anywhere.

| Bed | Pass | Force early → late [N] | Depth early → late [cm] | Pile in front at the end [cm] |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| narrow, 0.36 m | out, fresh sand | 703 → 1052 | 7.3 → 7.0 | 20.8 |
| narrow, 0.36 m | back, own track | 668 → 768 | 10.3 → 9.3 | 16.3 |
| wide, 1.6 m | out, fresh sand | 557 → 756 | 13.9 → 16.2 | 12.7 |
| wide, 1.6 m | back, own track | 464 → 609 | 16.7 → 16.7 | 9.7 |

In the narrow bed the force rises by half at constant depth and speed while a pile builds in front of the cube;
in the wide bed most of the raised sand lies beside the path (23 L beside, 13 L in front; narrow bed 7 L and
16 L). The pass through its own track needs less force in both beds.

**Limits.** The cube is held level, so it ploughs: the force is 3 to 7 times its weight, far above the roughly
105 N on the cube towed by G1, which is free to tilt and climb. These are not towing forces. Only 25 of the
120 L missing from the trench in the narrow bed reappear above the surface.

**Files** (unified worktree). Scripts `scripts/benchmarks/t3/box_channel.py`, `box_channel_figure.py`. Data
`logs/t3/t3-box-channel-2/t3/box_channel/` (OSMO workflow `t3-box-channel-2`). Figures and numbers
`logs/t3/box_channel_fig/box_channel.png`, `box_channel_map.png`, `box_channel.json`. To show it again, pass
`--box-channel logs/t3/box_channel_fig/box_channel.png` to `site_slides.py`.

### B. G1 tows the cube along a sand channel (2026-11-22 and 23)

Status: measured in this session, unchecked. MPM-trained tow policy, 15 kg cube unless stated, 16 trials per
row, 1.0 m/s command unless stated.

**Question.** What happens when the sand the cube displaces cannot leave sideways and G1 keeps pulling?

**Setup.** The towing test with two walls 0.36 m apart along the tow line. The walls act on the sand only; robot
and cube pass through them, and a guide force (4 to 6 N on average) keeps the cube on the line. "Open bed" is the
same run without walls. Force and depth are averages over 8 to 11 s; depth is the cube's bottom below the
settled sand surface, pile is the sand at the cube's front face above that surface.

| Cube | Bed | Goals | Cube travel [m] | Ground force on cube [N] | Tether tension [N] | Pile [cm] | Depth [cm] |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| free, 15 kg, 1.0 m/s | channel | 16 / 16 | 9.4 | 115 | 126 | 8.4 | 0.8 |
| free, 15 kg, 1.0 m/s | open | 16 / 16 | 9.5 | 103 | 115 | 6.5 | 1.8 |
| free, 15 kg, 0.5 m/s | channel | 16 / 16 | 9.5 | 96 | 109 | 6.1 | 0.9 |
| free, 15 kg, 0.5 m/s | open | 16 / 16 | 9.5 | 93 | 104 | 5.0 | 2.2 |
| held level, 15 kg | channel | 15 / 16 | 1.4 | 122 | 138 | 4.7 | 7.8 |
| held level, 15 kg | open | 15 / 16 | 1.5 | 135 | 151 | 3.9 | 10.2 |
| free, 20 kg | channel | 15 / 16 | 8.5 | 155 | 170 | 7.5 | 2.1 |
| free, 20 kg | open | 16 / 16 | 8.9 | 166 | 183 | 5.4 | 5.4 |
| free, 25 kg | channel | 13 / 16 | 3.0 | 161 | 177 | 4.8 | 5.5 |
| free, 25 kg | open | 9 / 16 | 4.4 | 168 | 184 | 3.4 | 6.9 |
| free, 30 kg | channel | 13 / 16 | 0.7 | 122 | 136 | 2.6 | 9.5 |
| free, 30 kg | open | 15 / 16 | 0.6 | 106 | 119 | 1.7 | 11.1 |

"Goals" counts the robot's path length, which also grows when it rocks in place, so for the level and the heavy
cubes the cube travel is the number to read.

**Result.** With the free 15 kg cube the channel raises the pull by about a tenth at 1.0 m/s (115 against 103 N)
and by 4 % at 0.5 m/s. More sand collects in front of the cube, but the cube is free to rise and rides over it
(0.8 cm deep against 1.8 cm). Held level, or made heavier, the cube digs in (8 to 11 cm) and G1 stalls after
0.6 to 1.5 m; the channel makes little difference then. The tether rises at about 25° and carries about 50 N of
the cube's 147 N weight; the free cube tilts about 6°.

**Limits.** The walls are not physical for the robot or the cube. One training seed, one checkpoint.

**Files** (unified worktree). Scripts `scripts/benchmarks/t3/tow_channel.py`, `tow_channel_figure.py`,
`sand_pile_figure.py`. Data `logs/t3/t3-tow-channel-4` (channel and open, 1.0 and 0.5 m/s), `-7` (free and level
with particle snapshots), `-8` (box-only videos and particles, level video), `-9` (20, 25, 30 kg), `-10`
(closer-camera videos). Figures and numbers `logs/t3/tow_channel_fig/` (`tow_channel.png`,
`tow_channel_across.png`, `tow_channel_level.png`, the `.json` files, and `free/`, `level/`, `mass20/`,
`mass25/`, `mass30/`), `logs/t3/sand_pile_fig/`, videos `logs/t3/tow_channel_video/`. To show it again, pass
`--tow-channel logs/t3/tow_channel_fig/tow_channel.png --tow-channel-video logs/t3/tow_channel_video
--tow-level logs/t3/tow_channel_fig/tow_channel_level.png --sand-pile logs/t3/sand_pile_fig/sand_pile.png
--tow-mass logs/t3/tow_channel_fig --sand-pile-mass logs/t3/sand_pile_fig/sand_pile_mass.png` to `site_slides.py`.
<!-- T3-box-test:end -->

## Superseded: P2 object towing, the start-up peak in the ground force on the cube (2026-11-23)

**Superseded on 2026-11-24.** The peak below (1.15 to 1.21 times the steady force) was measured on the tow
checkpoint of iteration 30,500. On the final tow checkpoint (39,998; T2 logs `logs/t2/tow_d25`, `tow_d35`,
`tow_d45`; MPM-trained tow policy on MPM sand, 16 trials each) the peak of the mean ground force in the first
4 s against its mean from 5 to 10 s is, at the 1.0 m/s command, 1.11 on the 0.25 m bed (116 against 105 N),
1.02 on 0.35 m (122 against 120 N) and 1.08 on 0.45 m (132 against 122 N); at the 0.5 m/s command 1.11, 1.00
and 1.01. Recomputed in this session with the same definition; the independent check of the towing rerun
(`checks/T2.md`) finds the same three values at 1.0 m/s. So on the final checkpoint there is no start-up peak
worth a statement, and the peak is not used on the slides or in the paper. The text below is kept as a record.


Status: read from existing logs, no new runs, unchecked. MPM-trained tow policy, 15 kg cube, 1.0 m/s, 16
trials each: the towing evaluation on MPM sand and on rigid ground (`logs/t2/tow_eval2`), and the open-bed run
of the channel test (`logs/t3/t3-tow-channel-4`), the only towing log that recorded the sand surface. Script
`scripts/benchmarks/t3/tow_startup_figure.py`; figure and numbers `logs/t3/tow_startup/` (unified worktree).

| Quantity (mean over trials, 0.2 s average) | Towing evaluation, MPM sand | Open bed, MPM sand | Rigid ground |
|---|---|---|---|
| Peak ground force in the first 4 s [N], at time [s] | 119 at 2.38 | 125 at 2.34 | 55 at 2.44 |
| Steady ground force, 5–10 s [N] | 103 | 103 | 50 |
| Peak / steady | 1.15 | 1.21 | 1.11 |
| Cube speed at the peak / steady [m/s] | 0.73 / 0.91 | 0.65 / 0.90 | 1.01 / 1.00 |
| Cube centre below its rest level on the settled surface, at the peak / steady [cm] | 3.5 / 1.8 | 3.9 / 1.8 | — |
| Cube tilt at the peak / largest (time) / steady [deg] | 8.1 / 8.7 (2.54 s) / 5.9 | 6.2 / 8.5 (2.78 s) / 5.6 | 1.9 / 2.7 (0.84 s) / 1.8 |
| Sand at the front face above the settled surface, at 1 s / at the peak / at 4 s / steady [cm] | not recorded | 2.1 / 5.6 / 6.2 / 6.4 | — |
| Drift of the force from 4 s to the end [N/s] | −0.17 (4.0–11.3 s) | −0.14 (4.0–11.3 s) | −0.10 (4.0–10.2 s) |

**Reading.** The peak comes with the cube digging in: at the moment of the peak the cube is at its deepest
(3.5 to 3.9 cm below its rest level, against 1.8 cm in steady towing) and at its slowest (0.65 to 0.73 m/s,
against 0.9). It is not the jerk of the tether going taut, which happens 1.5 s earlier (tension first reaches
100 to 120 N at about 0.8 s; on rigid ground 82 N at 0.64 s). After that jerk the cube overshoots to about
1.05 m/s, the tension eases to about 95 N between 1.2 and 1.9 s, and the cube sinks and slows; the force peak is
where the tether pulls it out again (tension 134 to 140 N at 2.4 s). It is also not a pile that builds and then
goes away: the sand at the front face is still rising at the peak (5.6 cm) and stays at 6.2 to 6.4 cm afterwards.
The tilt is largest slightly after the peak. The logs hold the size of the tilt but not its direction, so
nose-down cannot be told from nose-up. The depth is that of the cube's centre, which tilting alone would raise,
not lower, so the extra 2 cm are real sinking; the lowest edge may be up to 2 cm deeper than this number at 8.5°.

**Steady force.** It does not drift upward: the straight-line fit from 4 s to the last sample with at least
half of the trials running falls by 0.14 to 0.17 N/s on MPM sand (about 1 N over 7 s).
<!-- T3-tow-startup:end -->

## Superseded: do the loaded feet reach the rigid base under the sand? (2026-11-24, first pass)

**Superseded by the final rerun of 2026-11-24.** This block describes the first pass (MPM at iteration 16,500,
0.25 and 0.35 m beds) and uses "within 2 cm of the base" as a validity rule. The final rerun tested that rule on
0.25, 0.35 and 0.50 m beds and dropped it: it does not pick out the cells that change with bed depth (block
"P1-1 terrain geometry", part "Bed depth"). The clearance itself is still reported for every cell there. The base
height in the numbers below is also wrong near the edge of the start platform, as the independent check found;
runs from the final rerun on read the mesh height under each sole corner. Kept as a record.


Status: read from the logs of the walking evaluation, unchecked. Results in which the feet reach the base are results on rigid ground and are invalid as material or slope results. Script `scripts/benchmarks/t3/base_clearance.py`; numbers `logs/t3/base_clearance/base_clearance.json`.

**Rule.** For every loaded sample the height of the lowest of the four sole corners above the rigid base is taken (the base is the terrain mesh lowered by the bed depth). The feet stop about 1.3 cm above the base, one layer of sand particles, so a corner within 2 cm counts as standing on the base. Each cell: trials in which that happens, the median over trials of the closest approach, and the share of loaded samples within 2 cm.

## P1-1 terrain geometry: slope sweep on firm sand (2026-11-26)

Status: measured in this session; the recheck of the final rerun is in `checks/T3.md`, the runs made after it are unchecked. Three policies: MJWarp `3qymp37c`, RFT `6t0h2ud8`, MPM `aga5ovkp` (iteration 19,999 (final)). The earlier MPM policy `eayrj79r` is no longer shown because it saw sand only on wave and random rough terrain; its results stay on disk (`logs/t3/eval_5`, `logs/t3/flow_1`).

Without the shin-foot stop, with sleeping sand and the 1.2 m window: MJWarp walks +10°, not +15°; RFT climbs to +30°, the MPM checkpoint to +25°. Without sleeping MJWarp walks +15° and fails from +20° (1.2 m window; see limits).

With the stop: downhill, every policy reaches the goal; MPM tracks the command worst.

With the stop, the +30° result holds without the start drop and with the sand kept awake.

<b>Final rerun, 2026-11-24, not yet independently checked.</b> Beach sand (1800 kg/m³, friction 0.80, no cohesion), 1.0 m/s command, 13 s, slopes −30° to +30° in 5° steps, 0.50 m bed (the training bed is 0.25 m). 10 trials per policy and slope: seeds 41 and 42 with 5 trials each, because 10 trials on this bed do not fit in the memory of one L40. MJWarp <code>3qymp37c</code>, RFT <code>6t0h2ud8</code> and MPM <code>aga5ovkp</code>, each at its final checkpoint (iteration 19,999), run together in every workflow from one code bundle.

<b>Goals of 10 at +10°, +15°, +20°, +25°, +30°.</b> MJWarp 4, 0, 0, 0, 0; RFT 10, 10, 10, 10, 3; MPM 10 at each. With 40 trials per cell RFT reaches 39 of 40 at +25° and 13 of 40 at +30°, MPM 40 of 40 at each: MPM and RFT are separated at +30° (Fisher's exact test, p = 2e-11) and not at +25° (p = 1). Downhill every policy reaches every goal.

<b>Velocity error of the successful trials.</b> Uphill MPM is lowest (0.06–0.07 m/s up to +20°, 0.13 m/s at +30°; RFT 0.10–0.13 and 0.18 m/s). On steep downhill slopes MPM is highest: 0.28, 0.20 and 0.14 m/s at −30°, −25° and −20°, against 0.22, 0.14 and 0.10 m/s for RFT.

<b>On rigid ground the order reverses</b> (same slopes, no sand, same bundle, 10 trials): at +30° MJWarp reaches 9 of 10, RFT 8 and MPM 0; at −30° MJWarp 9, RFT 7 and MPM 5. Between −20° and +15° all three reach 10 of 10. Each policy is best on the kind of ground it trained on.

<b>Does the result depend on how the test is set up?</b> Three controls on the steep slopes, 40 trials per policy each (table below): the robot started on the settled surface instead of dropped onto it, the same with 6 s settling, and with 12 s settling and all sand near the robot kept awake. MPM reaches 40 of 40 at every slope in every setting. RFT reaches 39 or 40 of 40 up to +25° in every setting and 13, 9, 18 and 1 of 40 at +30°. MJWarp reaches 0 of 40 from +20° in every setting; at +15° it reaches 0 of 40 with the task's sleeping and 23 of 40 with the sand awake, so where MJWarp starts to fail (between +10° and +20°) depends on the setting. RFT's success rate at +30° is not a stable number either: it ranges from 1 to 18 of 40 over the settings, and only its separation from MPM holds in all of them. RFT's velocity error at +25° is 0.16 m/s in the task setting started on the surface and 0.26 m/s with the sand awake, more than the 0.05 m/s tolerance, so no statement on RFT's error at +25° and above is made from one setting. The figures above use the default start, 2.5 s settling and the task's sleeping. The sand is generated with 1.25 particles per cell edge in every run; at that density the bed compacts while it settles (task record), which is a condition of every sinkage and sand-motion number.

<b>What a trial is.</b> Within one run all trials share one settled bed (one template) and differ only in the robot's start: position within ±0.1 m, heading within ±0.1 rad, joint angles within ±10 %. The starts are the same for all three policies. Forty trials are forty starts on that bed, not forty beds.

<b>Limits.</b> One training seed and one checkpoint per policy. The start states on the 0.50 m bed are not the same ten as on the other beds. The robot starts 0.76 m above the level the sand is generated at and the settled surface of this bed lies 10 cm below it, so every trial starts with a 10 cm drop onto the flat start platform, 1.9 m before the slope; on level ground of this bed all 30 trials reach the goal, and the uphill failures happen 3 to 7 m from the start. MJWarp's numbers depend on the bed depth (block below).

<b>Training budget, from the W&B configs.</b> MJWarp and RFT: 1024 environments on each of 4 GPUs, 24 steps per iteration, 20,000 iterations, about 1.97 billion transitions each. MPM: 64 environments on each of 40 GPUs, 24 steps, 20,000 iterations, about 1.23 billion transitions. This reads the configured environment count as per GPU, which is not verified beyond the config. All three use training seed 42.

**Bed depth: why the 0.50 m bed, and what changes with depth.**

The slope sweep and the six sands of the material axis (level ground and +10°) were run on 0.25, 0.35 and 0.50 m beds. Rule fixed before the runs: a cell counts as unchanged between 0.35 and 0.50 m if its goal count differs by at most 3 of 10 and its velocity error by at most 0.05 m/s. Of 75 cells, 13 changed: 12 of MJWarp, 0 of RFT and 1 of MPM (table). MJWarp always does worse on the deeper bed.

The earlier validity rule (a cell is invalid if any trial comes within 2 cm of the rigid base) does not pick out the cells that change: 10 of the 13 changed cells pass it, the changed cells reach up to 14.7 cm of clearance, and 44 unchanged cells lie at or below that. As decided beforehand for this case, clearance is dropped as a validity rule and results are quoted from the deepest bed. For RFT and MPM that bed is deep enough by this test; for MJWarp no bed tested is: where it has not already failed, it could do worse on a deeper bed.

On the 0.35 m bed the feet come within 2 cm of the base at +30° in 36 of 40 MPM trials and 33 of 40 RFT trials; on the 0.50 m bed in 1 of 40 MPM trials (1.9 cm) and no RFT trial. The goal counts at +30° are the same on both beds (RFT 10 and 13 of 40, MPM 40 and 40 of 40).

<!-- T3-yield-material:start -->
## P1-2 terrain material: from hard ground to loose poppy seed, level ground (2026-11-26)

Status: measured in this session; the recheck of the final rerun is in `checks/T3.md`, the runs made after it are unchecked. Three policies: MJWarp `3qymp37c`, RFT `6t0h2ud8`, MPM `aga5ovkp` (iteration 19,999 (final)). The earlier MPM policy `eayrj79r` is no longer shown because it saw sand only on wave and random rough terrain; its results stay on disk (`logs/t3/eval_5`, `logs/t3/flow_1`).

MPM against MJWarp is supported: MJWarp falls on loose poppy seed in both bed settings.

With the stop, MPM and RFT reach every goal and RFT's velocity error is 0.03–0.08 m/s higher on every sand; without it, on beach sand and loose poppy seed, both reach every goal and RFT's error is the lower one.

On the ladder that gap does not grow with softness; on the grid the two settings disagree.

<b>Runs made after the recheck; checked (check 3 in <code>checks/T3.md</code>).</b> Level ground, 1.0 m/s, 0.50 m bed, 42 sands (beach sand with density and friction replaced), 5 trials per sand and policy, three policies per task. Unlike in the earlier grid, every sand is generated and settled as itself, and the robot starts 0.76 m above that sand's settled surface. Setting T (the task's own): 2.5 s settling and the task's sleeping. The same grid in setting W (12 s settling, sand near the robot awake) is in a block below. The figure keeps its earlier style.

<b>Grey crosses: the bed collapses while settling.</b> How far the bed sinks before the run depends on the friction only, not on the density: 8, 12, 15, 21, 25 and 30 cm of the 50 cm bed for friction 0.9, 0.7, 0.5, 0.3, 0.2 and 0.1 in setting T (10, 14, 18, 27, 33 and 38 cm in W; with friction 0.1 most sands could not be run at all). A sand counts as collapsed when its surface lies more than a third of the bed depth below the generated level, a rule fixed before the runs: friction 0.3 and below in T, friction 0.5 and below in W. The threshold was chosen after probing friction 0.1, 0.3 and 0.9 (30, 21 and 8 cm) and before the 15 and 18 cm of friction 0.5 were known, which it ends up dividing. Collapsed sands are not material results, which also covers the earlier "feet reach the base on low friction". The 2 cm marks of the earlier figure are gone with the rule.

<b>On the 14 sands whose bed holds in both settings</b> (friction 0.7 and 0.9, 250 to 3000 kg/m³): MPM reaches 70 of 70 goals in T and in W with a velocity error of 0.05–0.12 m/s per sand; RFT reaches 70 of 70 in both with 0.09–0.23 m/s (T) and 0.09–0.18 m/s (W); MJWarp reaches 54 of 70 (T) and 65 of 70 (W) with errors up to 0.68 and 0.41 m/s. The two settings agree, by the tolerance fixed beforehand, on 14 of 14 sands for MPM, 13 of 14 for RFT and 9 of 14 for MJWarp. RFT's error is above MPM's on every one of these sands. In T the difference grows towards light sand: 0.03–0.05 m/s from 1000 kg/m³ up, 0.05–0.06 at 500 and 0.08–0.13 at 250 kg/m³. In W there is no trend (0.04–0.08 m/s, largest at 3000 kg/m³). The settings disagree, so no statement on a trend is made from the grid; both policies' errors about double at 250 kg/m³, which lies outside the training range. Hard ground for reference: 0.04, 0.05 and 0.04 m/s.

<b>Friction 0.5 holds in setting T only</b> (15 cm in T, 18 cm in W), so it is not among the 14 sands above. In T, on those seven sands, MPM reaches 35 of 35 goals, RFT 34 of 35 (one fall at 1000 kg/m³) and MJWarp 16 of 35. The title of the figure counts the 21 sands that hold in T.

<b>What a trial is.</b> Within one run all trials share one settled bed (one template, terrain and scene seed fixed) and differ only in the robot's start: position within ±0.1 m, heading within ±0.1 rad, joint angles within ±10 %. The grid uses the same five starts on all 42 sands, in both settings and for all three policies, so "70 of 70" is 14 sands times those five starts. Comparisons between policies and between the settings are therefore paired; the spread between trials is small for the same reason and says little about other beds.

<b>Runs made after the recheck; checked (check 3 in <code>checks/T3.md</code>).</b> Level ground only, 1.0 m/s command, 13 s. Grounds fixed before the runs: hard ground (rigid contact); dense sand (3000 kg/m³, friction 0.90); beach sand (1800, 0.80); packed poppy seed (682, 1.07); light sand (1000, 0.70); loose poppy seed (640, 0.73). All sands are cohesionless with rigid grains, generated with 1.25 particles per cell edge. They are ordered by measured foot sinkage, the same order in both settings. The two poppy seeds lie outside the range MPM was trained on. 0.50 m bed, 10 trials per policy and ground (seeds 41 and 42: ten robot starts on one settled bed per run, the same starts for every policy and in both settings), three policies per workflow, robot started 0.76 m above the settled surface, velocity error from 1.0 s over the trials that reached the goal.

<b>Two bed settings, and statements only where they agree.</b> How far a foot sinks in this simulation depends on how the bed was prepared (block below), so the ladder was run twice: in setting T (2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping; shown above) and in setting W (12 s settling, all sand near the robot awake; block below). A statement is made only where both agree within 3 goals of 10 and 0.05 m/s.

<b>What holds in both settings.</b> MPM reaches 10 of 10 goals on every ground with a velocity error of 0.04–0.08 m/s. RFT reaches 10 of 10 on every ground with 0.05 m/s on hard ground and 0.09–0.13 m/s on every sand. MJWarp falls in 10 of 10 trials on loose poppy seed, and on beach and dense sand it reaches every goal. So MPM against MJWarp is supported. MPM against RFT is not separated in goals anywhere; RFT's error is 0.03–0.08 m/s above MPM's on every sand, firm or soft, and 0.01 m/s above on hard ground. That is a small constant gap, not a sign that MPM copes better with softer ground.

<b>What depends on the setting.</b> MJWarp on the two grounds in the middle: on light sand it falls in 10 of 10 trials in T and reaches 10 of 10 in W (with 0.39 m/s error); on packed poppy seed it reaches 9 of 10 with 0.48 m/s in T and 10 of 10 with 0.06 m/s in W. Sinkage is also smaller in W on every sand (dense sand 2.1–3.1 cm against 4.2–5.0 cm; loose poppy seed 15–24 cm against 21–27 cm).

<b>How each policy steps, in both settings.</b> MJWarp's sole tips further toes-down in mid-stance the softer the ground: 14°, 25° and 31° from dense sand to packed poppy seed in T, and 8°, 16°, 24° and 36° from dense to light sand in W. On loose poppy seed it is 58° (T) and 48° (W), but those are angles of a falling robot: every trial there ends within 4 s and the values come from the few steps taken from 1.5 s until 0.5 s before the fall (8 steps in T). Both feet are loaded for more of the time (up to all of it) and the pelvis drops. RFT and MPM keep the sole near level (RFT at most 10° toes-down in T and 6° in W, MPM within about 4° of level) and both shorten the step to 0.22–0.24 s on the softest ground. Their feet sink equally deep. On the three softest grounds MPM keeps the foot on the ground longer than RFT (stance 0.28–0.38 s against 0.25–0.26 s), with a longer double stance (0.05–0.07 s against 0.01–0.03 s); on hard ground, dense sand and, in setting W, beach sand RFT's stance is as long as MPM's or longer. MPM's peak load falls with softness to about 1.15 body weights while RFT's stays at 1.3–1.5.

<b>Answer by the rule fixed beforehand: not explained yet.</b> A quantity was to be named only if it follows the velocity error across the grounds for both baselines (rank correlation of 0.8 or more) while MPM changes it less than half as much. None passes in either setting, because RFT's error does not vary with softness. MJWarp's rising error goes with its toes-down sole and its wading (rank correlation 1.0 in T, 0.7 in W). The earlier observation that MPM keeps a 0.33 s step where the baselines shorten theirs holds on beach sand in setting T (0.30 s against 0.25 and 0.22 s) and not in setting W (0.33 s against 0.32 and 0.28 s); on softer grounds MPM shortens its step too. It depends on the bed and is not the explanation.

<b>Tangential foot load, recomputed (unchecked).</b> The task's foot force is read once per control step (20 ms), after the last of its four physics steps. The runs also stored the sand's force on each foot after every physics step. Read once per control step, the vertical load is within 2 % of its mean over the physics steps, but the horizontal load is up to 14 % lower, unevenly between the policies. The peak tangential load of a stance and the tangential / normal ratio are therefore now taken from every physics step; the earlier values are kept beside them. Hard ground has only the contact sensor's record at the control rate, so its two values are the same. Peak normal load and loading rate are still read once per control step. No statement on this slide rests on these two columns.

<b>Requested by Junnosuke: does a policy degrade on softer ground because more sand flows out from under the foot?</b> Status: the fourth independent check (2026-11-24) reproduced every number from the raw downloads and passed with caveats on the method and the wording; this text is reworded as it asked. The values as volume of the settled bed and the values without the side region taken off were added afterwards; the column flow as volume of the settled bed agrees with the checker's own recomputation to within 0.02 L except for MJWarp on light sand (0.76 against 0.84 L, 15 footprints). These added values have not been checked again. Level ground, 1.0 m/s, 0.50 m bed, the ladder's grounds. A trial is one start of a robot; each robot starts on its own copy of the same settled sand (position ±0.1 m, yaw ±0.1 rad, joints ±10 %); 10 trials per policy and ground (seeds 41 and 42), three policies per workflow, sand generated with 1.25 particles per cell edge. Headline setting W (12 s settling, all sand near the robot awake); setting T (2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping) is in the block below. The runs repeat the ladder with the same seeds and a tracker that measures every footprint; goals and velocity errors agree with the ladder above within the tolerance (MJWarp on light sand reaches 9 of 10 here against 10 of 10). 170 to 247 footprints per cell in W; MJWarp gives 15 on light sand (from 7 of its 10 trials, taken only when a foot unloaded) and none on loose poppy seed, because its feet never unload there (it wades) or it falls.

<b>When the criterion was written.</b> The measures and the criterion are dated 12:59, 13:07 and 13:15 in the task record, the runs were downloaded between 14:06 and 14:11, the script that holds the criterion was last saved at 14:14, and the task record was first committed with these entries at 14:18, after the results. The stated order (criterion first) is consistent with these times but cannot be proven from the files.

<b>How flow and compaction are separated.</b> At touchdown the tracker remembers the sand around the sole; when the same foot lands again (about 0.5 s later) it looks at the same sand. <i>Column</i>: the vertical column under the sole rectangle, through the whole bed. Rut volume = sole area times the drop of the sand top; flow = sand that left the column sideways (particles before minus after); compaction in the column = rut minus flow. <i>Region</i>: the sole rectangle grown by 10 cm (39 by 27 cm; the other foot lands 3 cm outside it), with the sand top mapped on 2.5 cm cells: what sank = what rose + what left the region sideways + compaction. Compaction is defined as the remainder, so this budget closes by construction: there is no residual and no independent test that it closes. All values are sums or averages over the whole sole or region, not at one contact point.

<b>Unit.</b> A particle count becomes litres through the particle volume at the material's nominal density, so the flow in "litres at nominal density" is a measure of mass. The settled bed is denser than nominal: in the column under the sole at touchdown a particle takes up 0.67–0.83 of its nominal volume in W (MJWarp on light sand: 0.90, from 15 footprints) and 0.70–0.81 in T: the bed is 1.2 to 1.5 times its nominal density. The volume of settled bed that left is therefore smaller than the litres at nominal density, by this factor, and the compaction is larger by the same litres. Both are given below. The figure and the first table column use litres at nominal density; comparisons between policies on one ground and all rank orders do not depend on the choice.

<b>The region to the side is not undisturbed.</b> The same budget is taken in a region of the same size 0.35 m to the outer side, where no foot lands, and taken off in the middle and right panels. It is not the bed's own change: in W it sinks 0.44–0.85 L (4 to 8 mm) while the robot passes and loses almost no sand (0.0–0.1 L), and the undisturbed surface ahead of the robot does not drift over a trial (the checker's finding). In T it gains 0.1–0.6 L of sand pushed out of the footprint region, on every sand except loose poppy seed. Taking it off therefore gives a lower bound of the step's compaction in W, and in T it adds the gain to the flow. The values without it are given beside the others.

<b>More sand does leave from under the foot on softer ground, for every policy.</b> Sand that left the footprint column per stance (MJWarp / RFT / MPM), in litres at nominal density: dense sand 0.16 / 0.17 / 0.18; beach sand 0.39 / 0.33 / 0.37; packed poppy seed 0.83 / 0.49 / 0.51; light sand 1.08 / 0.79 / 0.79; loose poppy seed none / 1.53 / 1.33. As volume of the settled bed: 0.12 / 0.13 / 0.13; 0.29 / 0.24 / 0.27; 0.65 / 0.38 / 0.40; 0.76 / 0.53 / 0.53; none / 1.03 / 0.92 L. The sand that lay within 3 cm under the sole moves down by 1.0–1.2 cm on dense sand and 8.8–9.8 cm on loose poppy seed between dug-in and lift-off (average over the sole), and outward by less than 1 cm.

<b>It does not come up, and around the footprint most of what sinks is compaction (setting W).</b> In the region around the footprint almost nothing rises (0.00–0.08 L per stance) and the surface of the 8 cm band around the footprint drops by 1 to 4 cm. With the side region taken off, a fifth to a third of what the foot makes sink leaves the region sideways (0.13–0.14 L on dense sand, 1.0–1.2 L on loose poppy seed) and 65–83 % is compaction (0.45–0.49 L on dense sand, 3.1–3.4 L on loose poppy seed). Without taking it off the compaction is 1.0–1.1 L on dense sand and 3.9–4.1 L on loose poppy seed, 72–84 % of what sinks, and 80–88 % with the flow counted as volume of the settled bed. The step's compaction lies between the two. This holds for the region, on every ground and for every policy that has footprints, in setting W. It does not hold for the column under the sole: there the flow is half or more of the rut in litres at nominal density and 37–70 % as volume of the settled bed. The near-zero column compaction on light sand and loose poppy seed in the table (0.00 and 0.01 L) comes from the unit, not from a rut made of flow alone; as volume of the settled bed it is 0.33–0.58 L. The checker counted the density rise directly: per footprint the column gets 4–5 % denser on dense sand and 17–20 % on loose poppy seed. A rise of that size through a 36 cm column in one step is not what grains do; on a bed generated with 1.25 particles per cell edge it is what the sampling itself can produce (T1's finding).

<b>Between the policies</b> the flow is the same on dense and beach sand. On packed poppy seed in W MJWarp moves 63–69 % more sand out of the column than RFT and MPM (0.83 against 0.49–0.51 L) and sinks 20–27 % deeper, while it does not fail there (10 of 10, 0.07 m/s); in the region its flow is not larger (0.44 against 0.47–0.48 L), so the larger column flow goes with its toe-down sole (−24°) and not with more sand leaving the neighbourhood. On light sand in W, where it fails (9 of 10, 0.38 m/s), it moves 37 % more (1.08 against 0.79 L; 15 footprints from 7 trials) and sinks 73–81 % deeper. On loose poppy seed RFT moves more out of the column than MPM (1.53 against 1.33 L) and less out of the region (1.04 against 1.21 L); the two are not separated.

<b>Does degradation follow flow? Not shown, and not separable from sinkage.</b> MPM does not degrade on any ground, and RFT's error is flat (0.10–0.13 m/s on every sand), so MJWarp is the only policy the test can speak about. The rule fixed beforehand asked for a rank correlation of 0.8 between flow per stance and degradation in both settings; it is not met. MJWarp: 0.70 in W over 5 grounds (hard ground to light sand; loose poppy seed has no footprint) and 1.00 in T over 4 grounds (hard ground to packed poppy seed; light sand and loose poppy seed have no footprint). A ground where MJWarp falls in every trial has no flow value and drops out, so the grounds where it fails completely never enter its own test. The 0.70 comes from one reversal: dense sand counts as a degradation for MJWarp (0.11 against 0.04 m/s on hard ground) and ranks above beach sand and packed poppy seed. RFT: 0.09 in W and 0.60 in T, over all 6 grounds. Flow and sinkage have the same rank order over the grounds for every policy, so on the same grounds sinkage gives the same values (0.70, 1.00; 0.09, 0.60); the 0.83 quoted earlier for sinkage in W used one more ground (6), not a better measure. The rank test therefore only says whether a policy's error rises with softness; it cannot tell flow from sinkage, rut or compaction, and with 4 or 5 grounds it has little power (1.00 over 4 grounds happens by chance once in 24).

<b>Between policies</b>, the worse one should also move more sand. Where this can be tested and MJWarp is the worse one it holds: light sand in W, packed poppy seed in T. It cannot be tested where it matters most: on loose poppy seed (both settings) and light sand (T) MJWarp has no footprint. It fails on dense sand, where MJWarp and RFT track 0.05–0.08 m/s worse than MPM (0.11 and 0.13 against 0.05 m/s) with the same flow (0.16–0.18 L), and on beach sand for RFT (0.11 against 0.05–0.06 m/s, 0.33 against 0.37–0.39 L). <b>What the data supports:</b> on the softest ground where it still steps (light sand in W, packed poppy seed in T) MJWarp moves about a third to a half more sand out of the footprint column and sinks 40–80 % deeper than RFT and MPM; where it falls in every trial nothing could be measured; and on dense and beach sand the policies move the same sand while MJWarp and RFT track worse than MPM, so flow does not account for that gap.

**The same in setting T (2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping).**

In setting T the bed is looser and more sand leaves the footprint column. Per stance (MJWarp / RFT / MPM), in litres at nominal density: dense sand 0.34 / 0.37 / 0.38; beach sand 0.89 / 0.73 / 0.68; packed poppy seed 1.42 / 0.91 / 1.09; light sand none / 1.38 / 1.48; loose poppy seed none / 2.02 / 1.42 (MPM on loose poppy seed rests on 78 footprints). As volume of the settled bed: 0.27 / 0.29 / 0.29; 0.66 / 0.54 / 0.51; 1.14 / 0.72 / 0.87; none / 0.96 / 1.02; none / 1.44 / 0.97 L. Almost nothing rises (0.00–0.16 L).

The region budget is less clean in T than in W. The region to the side gains 0.1–0.6 L of sand by count on every sand except loose poppy seed, so taking it off adds this gain to the flow: 0.9–2.8 L leave the region as reported in the figure, 0.7–2.3 L without taking it off. "Most of what sinks is compaction" is not supported in T as reported: compaction is 43–66 % of what sinks, on beach sand the flow is larger than the compaction (1.9–2.2 against 1.6–1.8 L), and on dense and light sand they are about equal. Without the side region taken off compaction is 59–78 % of what sinks (1.4–5.0 L), with the flow as volume of the settled bed 69–85 %. The flow out of the region does not rise steadily with softness in T (0.9–1.5, 1.9–2.2, 1.9–2.2, 2.3–2.8, 1.8–2.1 L from dense sand to loose poppy seed).

The test gives the same reading as in W: MJWarp 1.00 for flow over 4 grounds and 1.00 for sinkage on the same grounds, RFT 0.60 over 6; between policies it holds for MJWarp on packed poppy seed (9 of 10, 0.51 m/s; 30–56 % more sand out of the column, 43–57 % deeper; 129 footprints), fails on dense sand (RFT against MPM), and cannot be run on light sand and loose poppy seed, where MJWarp has no footprint.

| Loose poppy seed (640 kg/m³, 0.73) | MJWarp | 0 / 10 | 0.8 (10 of 10 trials within 2 cm) | — | 23.9 ± 0.3 | 0.22 ± 0.01 | 0.99 ± 0.01 | -48 ± 2 | 35.6 ± 1.3 |
| Loose poppy seed (640 kg/m³, 0.73) | RFT | 10 / 10 | 13.7 | 0.10 ± 0.00 | 15.7 ± 0.1 | 0.23 ± 0.00 | 0.42 ± 0.01 | -6 ± 0 | 62.9 ± 0.1 |
| Loose poppy seed (640 kg/m³, 0.73) | MPM | 10 / 10 | 15.1 | 0.06 ± 0.00 | 15.4 ± 0.1 | 0.24 ± 0.00 | 0.57 ± 0.02 | -1 ± 0 | 61.0 ± 0.1 |

**The grid figure with the collapsed beds left out (setting T).**

The same data as the figure above; the surfaces stop at the sands whose bed holds (friction 0.5, 0.7 and 0.9 in setting T).

**The density-friction grid with 12 s settling and the sand near the robot awake (setting W).**

Setting W, 5 trials per sand and policy. Beds with friction 0.5 and below collapse while settling (18 cm or more of 50 cm). On the sands with friction 0.7 and 0.9 see the numbers in the details of the figure above.

**How the bed is prepared decides how far the feet sink.**

A local test after the recheck (MJWarp on dense sand, 0.50 m bed, default start; task record, definitions block) shows two things acting together. The bed keeps losing height while it is awake: its surface lies 8.2, 9.1, 9.7 and 10.0 cm below the generated level after 2.5, 6, 12 and 24 s of settling, although the settling check passes each time. And sleeping freezes the bed in the state it has: on a bed frozen after 2.5 s a foot wakes loose sand that then compacts under it (4.0 cm sinkage, 17 L of sand displaced per step, against 2.4 cm and 4 L with the sand kept awake); on a bed frozen after 6 s or more the sleeping sand gives 0.6–0.8 cm, a third of the 1.7–2.1 cm of awake sand. The earlier ladder with 6 s settling was in that second state ("sand that does not yield") and is withdrawn.

The T1 session traces the compaction to the sampling density (1.25 particles per cell edge leaves 12 to 14 % of the grid cells empty at the start; unchecked, in its record). That would not depend on friction, and it would account for about 6 to 7 cm of a 50 cm bed. The beds here sink 8 cm at friction 0.9 and 30 cm at friction 0.1, the same for every density, so the low-friction collapse needs a second, friction-dependent cause; the bed spreading sideways toward its angle of repose is one candidate, not tested.

Consequence for reading this page: foot sinkage, sand displaced per step and sand motion under the sole are numbers for one bed preparation each. Goal counts and velocity errors of RFT and MPM are the same in both settings on every ground of the ladder; MJWarp's are not.

Snow and clay were not rerun. The first material axis (level ground and +10°, default start) and the ladders with 6 s settling on 0.35, 0.50 and 0.65 m beds are superseded by the runs above; their data stay under <code>logs/t3/final_1</code> and the earlier numbers are in the block "Final-checkpoint rerun of P1" of the task record. The 0.65 m ladder's result on loose poppy seed (MPM 12 of 12, RFT 9 of 12, not separated, p = 0.22) was measured with 6 s settling and is not repeated in the two settings here.

<!-- T3-body-compare:start -->
## P1, whole body: joints, torques and actions of the MPM and the RFT policy (2026-11-26)

Status: computed in this session from the rollouts of the final rerun, no new runs; checked on 2026-11-25 (check 7 in `checks/T3.md`, pass with corrections, applied here). Definitions and rule: last entry of the definitions block (written and committed before the joint records were read). Slides: [site/T3/index.html](../site/T3/index.html), slide "Beyond the feet". Data: `logs/t3/final_1/body/body_compare.json`; scripts `scripts/benchmarks/t3/body_compare.py`, `body_figures.py`, `body_slides.py`.

<b>Asked by Junnosuke:</b> is there something beyond the feet, in the joints, the torques or the actions, that explains why MPM does better than RFT? <b>Checked on 2026-11-25 (seventh check: every claim reproduced from the raw rollouts; wording corrected as it asked).</b> No new runs: the rollouts of the final rerun already hold positions, velocities, applied torques and actions of all 29 joints, the base state and the feet at 50 Hz, and the foot forces after every physics step. Conditions: uphill on beach sand at +20°, +25°, +30°, 40 trials per policy and slope, and level ground on the five sands of the material ladder, 10 trials per cell; 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s. Bed settings T (start on the settled surface, 2.5 s settling, the task's sleeping) and W (12 s settling, sand kept awake) carry the rule; the default start with the drop and 6 s settling are used for the test inside RFT's trials. A trial is one start of a robot; each robot starts on its own copy of the same settled sand, and the three policies get the same starts. Every trial is cut into gait cycles (touchdown to the next touchdown of the same foot, load above 20 % of body weight) and reduced to 79 numbers: angle, range, velocity, torque and power of the leg joints, waist and arms, work per metre, action changes and target errors, pelvis height, tilt and rates, pelvis over the stance foot, foot placement, swing height, timing, loading, and the reaction to a slip.

<b>The rule, written down and committed before the joint records were read</b> (task record, definitions block, 2026-11-25, 01:23). A quantity counts as an explanation candidate only if (a) it separates MPM from RFT by at least two pooled standard deviations over trials, with the same sign, in T and in W and in every cell (three slopes, or five sands), and (b) it goes with the outcome. For the slopes (b) is: among RFT's own trials at +30°, measured over the first four gait cycles on the slope only, the trials that later fall lie further on RFT's side than those that reach the goal (area under the ROC curve at least 0.70 pooled over the three settings with the task's sleeping, and above 0.5 in at least two of them). For the sands (b) is: within a policy, over its 50 trials, the quantity follows the trial's velocity error (rank correlation at least 0.5 in the direction of the difference, in T and in W).

<b>Slopes: 24 of 79 quantities separate the two policies, none goes with RFT's falls.</b> MPM puts the work into the knee: knee torque (RMS) 20.2 / 30.3 N m at +20° and 32.3 / 47.5 N m at +30° (RFT / MPM, setting T), and the knees do 0.26 / 0.48 of the positive joint work at +20° and 0.39 / 0.53 at +30°. RFT lifts its feet about twice as high (30.4 / 14.8 cm at +20°; the largest height of the centre of the sole above the undisturbed sand surface vertically under it during the swing, so it is the clearance above the slope, not the lift from where the foot left the ground; the step length is the same, see the block on the swing foot below) with a knee that bends through 121 / 87.7°, swings arms and waist more and sets the foot down ahead of the pelvis (6.89 / 0.53 cm). MPM rolls the pelvis more and uses the ankle roll joint (2.19 / 8.11 N m). At touchdown MPM pushes with the knee: its knee torque is about 115 N m there against 53 N m for RFT (the checker's values; Figure W1). Step length, step width, stance duration, pelvis pitch and height, hip torques, joint work per metre and action changes do not separate them, and there is no simpler gait difference at +30°: forward speed 0.975 / 0.961 m/s, duty factor 0.51 / 0.48, gait cycle 0.40 / 0.38 s, step width 17 / 17 cm, pelvis height 49 / 48 cm (RFT / MPM, from the seventh check). Three of the separating quantities are the slip-reaction ones; the check found that on these slopes the slip event as defined is every touchdown, so they measure the touchdown and not a reaction, and they carry no meaning of their own. Inside RFT's own trials at +30° (80 that do not reach the goal, of which 76 fall and 4 leave the lane, and 40 that reach it) none of the separating quantities reaches 0.70: the pooled values lie between 0.42 and 0.63, none is above 0.5 in all three settings (0 of 24), and 13 are above 0.5 in the two settings started on the surface and below it with the default start, so the settings do not agree even on the direction. In the first four gait cycles on the slope RFT's failing and successful trials are the same gait (Figure W1). What tips RFT at +30° is therefore not visible in how it steps early on the slope; it is not explained here.

<b>Sands: 25 of 79 quantities separate the two policies; 2 pass the rule as written, and I do not read them as explanations.</b> On sand RFT keeps a short stance and little double support (0.032 / 0.23 of the time on beach sand in T, 0.052 / 0.11 in W; RFT / MPM), and its ankle pitches through 30.1 / 4.77° per step there. MPM holds the ankle almost still on the firm sands only (range 3.15° on dense and 4.77° on beach sand, but 24.1° on light sand and 35.1° on loose poppy seed), and it shifts its weight with the hips (hip roll range 3.42 / 10.9°). The quantities that pass both parts are hip roll angle at mid-stance [deg] and shoulder roll mean angle [deg]: constant differences of a few degrees between the policies. Part (b) is weak on the sands: within a policy the velocity error hardly changes from sand to sand (RFT 0.09 to 0.13 m/s, MPM 0.05 to 0.08), so a correlation over the 50 trials is mostly a correlation with which sand it is. Tested one step further (added after seeing the two passes): Hip roll angle at mid-stance [deg]: within one sand, over its trials, the rank correlation with the velocity error is +0.24, -0.04, -0.07, -0.22 (T RFT, T MPM, W RFT, W MPM); on the density-friction grid the sign of MPM minus RFT holds on 14 of 14 sands in T and 14 of 14 sands in W; Shoulder roll mean angle [deg]: within one sand, over its trials, the rank correlation with the velocity error is +0.35, +0.18, -0.03, +0.21 (T RFT, T MPM, W RFT, W MPM); on the density-friction grid the sign of MPM minus RFT holds on 14 of 14 sands in T and 14 of 14 sands in W. Within one sand neither follows the velocity error, so they mark the policy, not the error.

<b>What RFT's higher velocity error on sand consists of (arithmetic, not an explanation).</b> The velocity error is the mean distance of the pelvis velocity from the command. On every sand RFT's forward speed is less steady: its spread about the trial mean is 0.060 / 0.038 m/s on dense sand and 0.13 / 0.075 m/s on loose poppy seed (RFT / MPM, T), against 0.023 / 0.019 m/s on hard ground. On the firmest sand RFT also walks slower than commanded (-0.11 / -0.037 m/s in T, -0.12 / -0.032 m/s in W). The sideways speed shows no consistent difference. Within one sand, the trials with a larger spread of the forward speed are the ones with a larger error, for both policies and settings (rank correlation +0.49, +0.60, +0.53, +0.46; T RFT, T MPM, W RFT, W MPM). So the gap is a less steady forward speed, which fits a gait with short stance and no double support, but which joint behaviour produces it was not isolated.

<b>Limits.</b> One training run and one checkpoint per policy. The trials of one run start on copies of the same settled sand and differ in the start only, so spreads are narrow and almost any difference between two controllers passes part (a); only part (b) carries weight. The test inside RFT's trials uses the first four gait cycles on the slope and cannot see what happens later. The centre of mass is not recorded; the pelvis stands in for it. Hard ground has no joint record. Target minus position mirrors the torque, because the joint drives are soft. The 6 s setting and the default start enter only the test inside RFT's trials.

**Videos of the three policies (rendered runs; checked in the tenth check).** With the stop, same start: RFT lifts its feet about twice as high as MPM.

<b>What "MPM takes lower steps" means (asked by Junnosuke; checked on 2026-11-25, tenth check: every number reproduced; two remarks of the check are worked in).</b> The swing foot height is the height of the centre of the sole above the undisturbed, settled sand surface vertically under the foot at that moment, largest value over the swing. So it is the clearance above the local slope surface: not the height above the stance foot and not the lift from where the foot left the ground. A swing is the time a foot carries less than 20 % of body weight between two of its stances. On the slopes (beach sand, default bed setting, +20°, +25°, +30°; RFT / MPM) it is 30.4 / 14.6, 29.7 / 15.3, 24.6 / 14.9 cm. <b>Lower does not mean shorter:</b> the step length along the slope is 0.21 / 0.22, 0.21 / 0.21, 0.22 / 0.21 m and a step takes 0.22 / 0.21, 0.21 / 0.20, 0.20 / 0.19 s for both. The ground rises 7 to 11 cm per step, and the swing foot travels two steps, which is why the rise from the lift-off point is much larger than the clearance. Both policies swing the foot with the toes pointing down (the sole is pitched about 70° below horizontal for RFT and 50° for MPM at the top of the swing), so the lowest corner of the foot is far below its centre. For the foot's collision shape, a plate of 17 x 6 x 1 cm, which is what the sand sees, the lowest corner rises to 18.6 / 3.7, 17.7 / 3.8, 13.4 / 3.2 cm above the level of the undisturbed surface (the tenth check finds 2.7 to 3.5 cm for MPM with its own surface reference; for the 19 x 7 cm sole the walk test records it is 17.4 / 2.1, 16.6 / 1.9, 11.7 / 0.9 cm). These are heights above the undisturbed level, not distances to the sand: in the robot's track the sand surface lies 5 to 9 cm lower (particle close-ups, tenth check), so the MPM policy's foot does not come as close to the sand as these numbers suggest. The smallest value over the swing is reached at lift-off and touchdown (-7.2 / -7.5, -9.1 / -9.6, -13.0 / -13.5 cm, the same for both; default bed setting, in W it is 1 to 7 cm). In setting W (12 s settling, sand kept awake) the order is the same and MPM is 2 to 5 cm higher. All three tasks reward foot clearance with the same weight (5) and target (0.1 m), but against a different height reference: the MPM task takes the ground under the pelvis from one scanner plus the sand depth, the RFT and MJWarp tasks one scanner per foot. <b>Where it holds:</b> MPM steps lower than RFT on the three slopes and on the three softer level sands in both bed settings; on dense sand MPM steps higher in both, and on level beach sand the two settings disagree (second table). <b>Do people walk like this?</b> Neither policy does: both take 0.2 to 0.3 m steps three to five times a second, where a person at 1 m/s takes steps of about 0.6 m less than twice a second (textbook values, not looked up here). People carry the foot low: the lowest point of the toe clears level ground by 1 to 2 cm in mid-swing (gait literature, from memory; not verified in this session). By that measure the MPM policy's low swing is the more human one and RFT's is a high-stepping gait. MJWarp falls on these slopes before it takes three swings, except in 4 trials at +20°.

| Slope | Policy | Sole centre above the slope surface, largest over the swing [cm] | The same at right angles to the slope [cm] | Lowest sole corner above the surface, largest over the swing [cm] | Sole centre, smallest over the swing [cm] | Rise from the lift-off point to the top [cm] | Step length along the slope [m] | Rise of the ground per step [cm] | Time per step / swing [s] | Trials (swings) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
